A directory the broker creates inside it is its own and stays 0700 (2700 with the inherited setgid bit). `fsGroupChangePolicy: OnRootMismatch` keeps the kubelet from re-chmodding it on later mounts.
- **`ps`:** the slim image has none, and the sign-in check needs the caller's controlling terminal. On Linux, `proc.tty()` now names it from `/proc/<pid>/stat`'s `tty_nr` (`pts/0`), as the rest of `proc.py` already reads `/proc`. `ps` is left for other systems.
- **Stopping:** uvicorn handles SIGTERM with a graceful shutdown, then raises the signal again for the handler it replaced. `run_foreground` installs a no-op handler there, so the broker's own cleanup runs (the pidfile, the lock) and the exit status is 0. `docker stop` takes well under a second.
- **Publishing:** test.yml's `image` job builds the image for linux/amd64 on every PR that runs the tests, and runs `tests/image` against it. After a release, release.yml's `publish-image` calls `.github/workflows/image.yml`. It builds the release's tag for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64 (QEMU), and pushes `ghcr.io/amahpour/switchboard:<version>` with a provenance attestation (`mode=max`) and an SBOM. It also pushes `:latest` when the version is the newest tag, so rebuilding an old release never moves `latest` back. `release.py` moves the image pins in the deployment examples and docs/DEPLOY.md together with the install pins.

### 30.6 Tests
- **Unit** (`test_web_origin.py`): `WebOrigin.parse`, with good URLs and every kind of bad one; `web_settings` and its refusals, which happen before the home exists; the config keys; the flags and their variables; a port range; `start` passing the settings to its child and printing the reported URL; `tcp_listener`. Also, in `test_proc.py`, Linux tty names and a real session leader in a pty (named as `ps` names it, where there is a `ps`).
- **Integration** (`test_public_url.py`), a broker behind `https://sb.example.com`, reached as the proxy reaches it: Host (421 for everything else, the local name included), the sign-in link and the Secure cookie (with its refresh and its deletion), writes that need the public Origin, the CSP, the WebSocket's Host, Origin and cookie, `status` and `ping`, and `/healthz` in both modes with every near miss. There are also real broker processes, one with `--public-url --log-stdout` and one without, each stopped by SIGTERM with exit status 0.
- **The image** (`tests/image`, marker `image`, run by CI's `image` job), which puts it behind Caddy with TLS from Caddy's own CA:
  - it refuses to start without a public URL, and runs as uid 10001 with a 0700 home;
  - it passes `/healthz` and Docker's health check, and anything else at its own address is 421;
  - over https (the certificate is verified): the page with its CSP, a sign-in link from `docker exec -t`, and a Secure cookie;
  - Chromium signs in and connects over wss, a same-origin fetch creates a room, and a posted Markdown message renders;
  - `docker stop` exits 0 quickly and a room survives the restart;
  - it copes with a root-owned disk (Render) and with an fsGroup volume under the Kubernetes manifest's securityContext (read-only root, no capabilities, no-new-privileges), each started twice.

### 30.7 Not in this change
Your own machines' agents joining a hosted broker, and signing in without exec (#41). Other people and their agents (#24). Several brokers or Postgres (#35).

## 31. A hosted broker you use from the web UI alone (#41)

*Since §32 (#61), the owner is the admin of a team: the claim link is also a one-time password, and everyone else signs in too.*

§30 put the broker behind a platform's HTTPS, and left two gaps: signing in needed a shell inside the container (`docker exec … switchboard login`), and no agent could join it, since remote links (§27, since retired) went from the broker out over SSH, which a hosted broker can't do to a laptop behind a router. #41 closed both for **one owner**, you and your own machines, in three changes: the owner and their passkeys (§31.2 to §31.6, this section as first written); the dial-in link, where a machine of yours paired from its command line and dialed `wss://<public URL>/link` (§31.7, retired); and the machines in the web UI (§31.8, retired). Agents over HTTPS (§49) replaced the last two. Other people and their agents were #24. Everything happens in the web UI, apart from reading one line in the container's log, once.

### 31.1 Goals and non-goals
- **Claim, then passkeys.** A fresh hosted broker has no owner. It prints one claim link in its log; whoever opens it and creates a passkey is the owner, and from then on only the owner's passkeys sign in. No setup token in the deploy config, no password, no identity provider.
- **Exec stays owner access.** `switchboard login` through `docker exec`/`kubectl exec` keeps working after the claim. It is a recovery path that needs no reset, it gives a session and not a passkey, and whoever can exec into the pod can read and change its database anyway, so turning it off would change nothing about who controls the broker.
- **Recovery without a shell in the broker's data:** `SWITCHBOARD_RESET_OWNER`, once per value (§31.5).
- **A stolen session can't make itself permanent** (§31.4): adding a passkey (and later making an agent token, §49.1) needs a passkey check in the last five minutes.
- **The desktop is unchanged:** no claim flow, no passkeys, `switchboard login` as before. Passkeys are on only behind a public URL where WebAuthn can work.
- **Not here:** the full passkeys and sessions sheet (removing one passkey, the browser list), sign-in through Google or GitHub, other people.

### 31.2 Data model (schema v3)
The v1 to v2 machinery (§4) runs as it is, generalized to steps: a verified 0600 backup of the file as it was (`switchboard.db.v<its version>.bak`, never overwritten), then every step's statements up to the current version in one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, then `integrity_check`, the old tables' row counts unchanged and the new tables empty. A version-1 file takes both steps after one backup. A newer schema is refused as before. It adds:
- **`passkeys`:** `credential_id BLOB PRIMARY KEY`, `public_key BLOB` (COSE, CBOR-encoded), `sign_count`, `name`, `aaguid`, `created_at`, `last_used_at`.
- **`web_sessions.via TEXT`:** `login-link`, `claim` or `passkey:<name>`; a session made before schema 3 has none. The later sessions list reads it.
- **`meta` rows:** `owner_handle` (16 random bytes, hex: WebAuthn's `user.id` for the one owner, made at the claim), `owner_claimed_at`, and `reset_owner_applied` (the SHA-256 of the last `SWITCHBOARD_RESET_OWNER` value acted on).
- **`link_machines`** for §31.7, so the dial-in link needs no second migration (dropped by schema 29, #385): `name PK`, `key BLOB` (the machine's Ed25519 public key), `key_fp`, `facts` (JSON), `rooms`, `harnesses`, `created_at`, `approved_at`, `approved_via`, `removed_at`, `last_seen_at`.

`tests/fixtures/db/v0_6_5.sql` is a schema-2 database written by 0.6.5 (its store and engine, a remote member, a `remotes` row), the way `v0_2_0.sql` is a schema-1 one; `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v3.py` migrates it.

### 31.3 The claim
**When it is on.** Only with a public URL (`--public-url`, §30) that browsers treat as a secure context (https, or plain http on `localhost` and `*.localhost`; the image tests' `http://*.test` has none) whose host is a DNS name (a relying-party id can't be an IP address). Otherwise the broker logs at start that passkeys are off and why, prints no claim link, and `switchboard login` in the container's shell is the way in. `WebOrigin.public` says whether a public URL was given, `WebOrigin.secure_context()` and `passkeys_unavailable()` (`broker/passkeys.py`) decide the rest.

**The token.** At start, with passkeys on, no owner and no passkeys, the broker makes 32 random bytes, keeps only their SHA-256 in memory (`ClaimTokens`), and prints one line on stdout, the container's log: `switchboard isn't set up yet. Claim it (link works once, for 60 min): https://<public URL>/setup#t=<token>`. Never through the logger: `broker.log` keeps no secrets, so the line goes through `print`, and with `--log-stdout` the log line beside it says only that a link was printed. In test mode the link is also written to `run/test-claim-link` (0600), as the login token is to `run/test-login-token`, so the suite reads it from there.
- **After `#`, not `?t=`.** A browser never sends the fragment, so the token can't land in a proxy's or gateway's access log. That matters because, unlike a login link (spent the moment it is opened), a claim token stays valid until the passkey is created. `setup.js` reads it, removes it from the address bar at once (`history.replaceState`), and sends it in the body of `POST /api/setup/begin`.
- **A fresh link every 60 minutes while unclaimed,** and the old token dies. A read-only role in a deploy tool can read logs but usually can't restart pods, so waiting for a restart could leave you with no working link; you always use the newest line. A restart prints a new one too.
- **Single use,** compared in constant time, spent by the claim. Bad tokens are rate-limited like bad login links: one `login` event (`bad_claim`, with a count) and one log line per minute, and nothing is written for the rest.

**The ceremony.** `POST /api/setup/begin {token}` checks the token and binds it to one registration ceremony for five minutes: the `Fido2Server` state and a 16-byte owner handle, made now, kept on the server under an id that travels in an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict (Secure behind https) `switchboard_setup` cookie. A second browser that opens the same link meanwhile gets 409 (`busy`); the same browser again replaces its own ceremony. The answer is the creation options: `rp.id` the public URL's host without its port, `rp.name` `switchboard`, `user.name` the human's screen name, `residentKey: required`, `userVerification: required`, `attestation: none`, the algorithms ES256, EdDSA and RS256, no `excludeCredentials`. `POST /api/setup/finish {credential, name}` needs the ceremony cookie, verifies the registration with `Fido2Server.register_complete` (the challenge, the exact origin, the RP id hash, user presence and verification; nothing from the authenticator is trusted beyond that), stores the passkey and the owner in one transaction, spends the token, creates a session (`via=claim`) and posts the notice `switchboard claimed (passkey "MacBook Pro")`. A registration that fails verification drops the ceremony and leaves the token usable until it expires: the owner tries again from the same link.

**The page.** `GET /setup` serves `setup.html` while a claim link is live and otherwise redirects to `/`. The page names the passkey (a default from the platform), creates it, then offers the backup step: a second passkey from a phone or a security key (a managed laptop may block passkey sync, and a backup is how you get back in without a reset), or Skip. The backup uses the add-a-passkey routes of §31.4 under the claim's grace. A link with no token shows the bad-link step at once; a token the broker refuses shows it after the request. The sign-in page (`login.html`) asks `GET /api/auth/state` and, while a hosted broker is unclaimed, points at the log instead of `switchboard login`.

### 31.4 Passkeys: signing in, the fresh check, adding one
**Signing in.** `POST /api/passkey/begin` (no session) answers the request options: no `allowCredentials` (the browser offers the owner's discoverable passkeys; on another device its QR flow uses the phone) and `userVerification: required`. Nothing is kept on the server before a sign-in succeeds: the ceremony's state (the challenge, the requirement) goes in an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict `switchboard_passkey` cookie sealed with an HMAC key the broker makes at start (`Sealer`), with its expiry inside (five minutes). `POST /api/passkey/finish {credential}` checks the seal and the expiry, verifies the assertion against the stored passkeys with `Fido2Server.authenticate_complete` (the challenge, the exact origin, the RP id hash, presence and verification, the signature), applies the sign-count rule, and only then remembers the challenge as used (`UsedChallenges`, until it would have expired), so a sealed cookie can't sign in twice and an anonymous caller can't fill memory. It records `last_used_at` and the counter, creates a session (`via=passkey:<name>`) and posts `new web login (passkey "iPhone")`, the existing warning. The routes answer 403 with a short code (`no_ceremony`, `bad_credential`, `sign_count`, `replay`, `no_passkeys`) and count failures like bad login links (`bad_passkey`).

**The sign-count rule** (WebAuthn §7.2): unless both the stored and the received counter are 0 (many platform authenticators never count), the received one must be greater than the stored one. An equal non-zero counter is refused too, since a cloned authenticator sends the same one twice; the notice says the passkey may have been copied.

**The fresh check.** A session that adds a passkey must have passed a passkey check in the last five minutes (`BrokerState.passkey_checks`, in memory), or be the claim's own session within ten minutes of the claim (`claim_grace`), for the backup step. Otherwise `POST /api/passkeys/begin` answers 403 `reauth`, and the page first runs the sign-in ceremony from the signed-in browser: `/api/passkey/finish` with a valid session cookie confirms that session (`passkey_check` event, no new session, `reauth: true`) instead of making one. Without this, someone who stole a session could add their own passkey, which would survive Sign out everywhere; with it, Sign out everywhere (`POST /logout {"all": true}`) gets rid of an intruder who has only a session. The same check gates pairing and approving a machine in §31.7. A session from `switchboard login` never passes it on its own: the first passkey only ever comes from the claim.

**Adding one.** `POST /api/passkeys/begin` (a session with a fresh check, the broker claimed) answers creation options with the owner's handle and `excludeCredentials` naming every existing passkey, its state sealed in a `switchboard_passkey_add` cookie; `POST /api/passkeys {credential, name}` verifies and stores it, and posts `passkey added ("iPhone")`. Names are one printable line of at most 40 characters (`clean_name`). In the signed-in app a key button next to sign-off (shown when `GET /api/me` says `hosted`) opens the Passkeys sheet: how many there are, Add a passkey, and Sign out everywhere. The full list, with removing one, is later.

**In the browser.** `webauthn.js`, shared by the claim page, the sign-in page and the app, is the one script that talks to `navigator.credentials`: it turns the broker's JSON options into what the API takes (base64url to buffers) and the credential back into JSON, and posts with the same-origin rules `app.js` uses (`credentials: 'same-origin'`, `X-Switchboard: 1`, and the browser's own `Origin`). The sign-in page used to load no script at all; it now loads this one and `login.js`, same-origin under `script-src 'self'`, with no inline code and no token in any page (`tests/unit/test_web_static_lint.py`).

### 31.5 The owner reset
`SWITCHBOARD_RESET_OWNER` is the one step that needs ops access. At start, if it is set and its SHA-256 differs from `meta.reset_owner_applied`, the broker, in one transaction, deletes every passkey and every web session, clears the owner, puts every paired machine back to pending (whoever held the broker may have paired one; the new owner approves or removes each, §31.7), records the hash and logs `owner reset (SWITCHBOARD_RESET_OWNER): … this can't be undone`; then, being unclaimed, it prints a claim link. The same value at the next start does nothing, so a leftover variable is harmless across pod moves: under GitOps, removing it takes another change that may take days, while pods move between nodes routinely, and a variable that reset at every start would wipe the passkeys after each move. To reset again, change the value. A reset is irreversible by design.

### 31.6 Threat model (a delta to §30.4)
- **New on the internet:** `/setup` and the `/api/setup/*` and `/api/passkey/*` routes need no session. They are Origin-checked like every write, rate-limited on failure, and reveal nothing about the owner: `GET /api/auth/state` says only whether the broker is hosted, claimed, has passkeys and has a live claim link, and the sign-in options carry no names.
- **The claim window.** Anyone who can read the container's logs (a read-only role in the deploy tool, a log service the logs are shipped to) can claim before you, for up to 60 minutes per link. You would see "already used" on the newest link, and `SWITCHBOARD_RESET_OWNER` recovers. Bots can't read your logs, so the public internet is covered; the exposure is the minutes between the deploy and your claim.
- **A stolen session can't make itself permanent:** adding a passkey needs a passkey check in the last five minutes, so Sign out everywhere ends an intruder who has only a session. A stolen ceremony cookie is worth nothing without the authenticator, and nothing after five minutes.
- **A cloned authenticator** shows as a signature counter that didn't grow, and is refused with a notice.
- **Exec access to the container is owner access, always.** `switchboard login` through exec keeps working after the claim; it gives a session, not a passkey, so it passes no fresh check. Keep platform credentials away from your agents (§30.4).
- **Changing the domain** means the passkeys' relying party no longer matches: set them up again through the reset.
- **The desktop is unchanged:** no claim flow and no passkey route does anything there.

### 31.7 The dial-in link
**Retired in 0.38.0 (#385).** A machine used to pair with a hosted broker by a code (`switchboard join`, `POST /link/pair`), dial it over `wss://` (`/link`) and run its agents through a local satellite. An agent now connects over HTTPS with an agent token (§49.1) and nothing of switchboard's runs on its machine, so the dial-in link, the pairing codes, the machines' keys and the owner's approval of a machine are gone, with the `link_machines` table (schema 29). The last release that has them is v0.37.2.

### 31.8 Machines in the web UI
**Retired in 0.38.0 (#385)** with the dial-in link (§31.7): the sidebar's Remote machines, Add a machine and the Machines sheet are gone. A person's devices are their agent tokens, in Settings → Agents (§49.1).

### 31.9 Tests
- **Unit** (`test_passkey_pieces.py`): the claim token (random, hashed, an hour, single use, minting kills the old one, the ceremony bound to one browser for five minutes); the sealed state (the expiry inside, a tampered or foreign seal is nothing); used challenges; where passkeys can work; the sign-count rule; names. `test_db_migrate_v3.py`: the v2 fixture migrates with a `.v2.bak`, the fresh schema, a failed statement leaves the version-2 file intact, a v1 file takes both steps after one backup.
- **Integration** (`test_passkeys.py`, an in-process broker behind `https://sb.example.com` with a software ES256 authenticator, `tests/fakes/fake_authenticator.py`): the claim link and page; bad tokens rate-limited and the write rules; the whole claim and everything it sets; one ceremony at a time and what the registration refuses (another origin or RP id, no verification, no presence, malformed); the ceremony's and the token's expiry; the hourly rotation; signing in, with the replay, the tampered seal, another origin or RP id or challenge, an unknown credential, a signature by another key; the sign-count rule; the sign-in ceremony's expiry; the backup passkey under the grace and `excludeCredentials`; the fresh check (a stale one, a session from a login link, the re-auth from a signed-in browser, the check's own expiry); Sign out everywhere keeping the passkeys; exec login after the claim; the reset once per value, putting a machine back to pending; no claim flow behind `http://sb.test` or an IP address, nor on the desktop; a real broker process behind `http://sb.localhost` whose claim line is on stdout once and in no log file, with a fresh line after a restart.
- **Browser** (`tests/e2e/test_passkeys_ui.py`, Chromium with virtual authenticators through CDP): a bad link, a refused token, the claim from the link with the token gone from the address bar, the backup passkey on a second device, the passkeys sheet (a third passkey, no check asked under the grace), `/setup` after the claim, sign off, the passkey button, signing in with the phone's passkey, Sign out everywhere and back in; the sign-in page on a phone with a cancelled ceremony. Every page fails on a console error or a CSP violation.
- **The image** (`tests/image`): the claim line read from `docker logs`, opened in Chromium through the TLS proxy with a virtual authenticator, the claim, sign off and sign in again, with no `docker exec`; nothing more printed once claimed; still claimed after a restart, with no new link.
- **Screenshots:** `docs/media/ui_shots.py` adds the claim page, the backup step, the sign-in page (desktop and phone) and the passkeys sheet from an unclaimed hosted broker (`tests/ui_world.py`'s `HostedWorld`).

### 31.10 Implementation notes
- **python-fido2** (Yubico, BSD-2) is the WebAuthn library: its only requirement is `cryptography`, which the lock already had. `attestation: none` and an exact-origin check replace its public-suffix RP check; `allowed_algorithms` is pinned to the three above.
- **Chromium allows one internal virtual authenticator at a time;** the tests give a security key the `usb` transport.
- **Two claim links opened in the same tab:** `setup.js` leaves the page at `/setup`, so a second link is a same-document hash change that runs no script. Nobody does that outside a test; the test navigates away first.
- **`truststore` on WSL2** reads the Linux distribution's store, not Windows's: behind a TLS-inspection proxy, add its root certificate there (`/usr/local/share/ca-certificates/`, then `update-ca-certificates`), as the image test does in its machine container.

## 32. People: a team on one hosted broker (#61)

§31 made a hosted broker usable from the web UI alone, for **one owner**. This section lets a team share it: the owner (now called the admin) adds people by name, everyone signs in with a password or a passkey of their own, and everyone's machines dial in. It is the MVP of #61 for a team on a private network (a company VPC reached over a VPN), deployed by its admin through a GitOps tool.

### 32.1 Goals and the trust model
- **Everyone answers to everyone.** Every signed-in person is a human in every room: their messages are `kind=human` to every agent, they post under their own name, run every room command and pair, approve or remove machines. #61's design sketch split agents by owner (only its own person's messages as its user's); for this deployment the admin chose the opposite, "a safe space, not a SaaS", and that split is not built. Nothing in the delivery rules changes: a person's message is a human message.
- **The admin section is the admin's.** Adding people, a new one-time password for someone, and removing someone.
- **Three ways in** on the sign-in page: a name and a password, a passkey, and SSO shown as coming soon.
- **A one-time password, then your own.** The admin's first password is printed once in the log (the claim, §31.3, now a password rather than only a link); everyone else's first one comes from the admin. Either way, the first sign-in with it can do nothing but choose a password of their own, or a passkey instead.
- **Not in this version:** SSO (OIDC; since §38, Google), per-room membership, roles beyond admin and everyone else, per-person budgets, each agent answering to one person (#61's sketch, if a deployment wants it later).

### 32.2 Data model (schema v4)
```sql
ALTER TABLE messages ADD COLUMN sender_person_id INTEGER;    -- a human sender who isn't the admin
ALTER TABLE web_sessions ADD COLUMN person_id INTEGER;       -- whose session; NULL: the admin
ALTER TABLE passkeys ADD COLUMN person_id INTEGER;           -- whose passkey; NULL: the admin
ALTER TABLE link_machines ADD COLUMN person_id INTEGER;      -- who paired it (set since #178; NULL: the owner)
CREATE TABLE people(
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT NOT NULL COLLATE NOCASE,
  handle BLOB NOT NULL UNIQUE,                               -- their WebAuthn user id (16 random bytes)
  password_hash TEXT, must_reset INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1, password_expires_at REAL,
  created_at REAL NOT NULL, removed_at REAL);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX people_active_name ON people(name) WHERE removed_at IS NULL;
UPDATE meta SET value='4' WHERE key='schema_version';
```
- **NULL is the admin**, so the migration rewrites no row: every existing passkey, session and message stays the admin's. The admin has no `people` row; their password's hash is `meta.owner_password` (their WebAuthn handle stays `meta.owner_handle`), and their name is `human_name`. On a desktop broker every session is NULL, as before.
- The same machinery as §27.6 and §31.2: a verified 0600 backup (`switchboard.db.v3.bak`), one transaction, the checks. Tested on a dump written by 0.7.0's own store (`tests/fixtures/db/v0_7_0.sql`).
- **A removed person** keeps their row, so their messages keep their name; their password, passkeys and sessions are deleted in the same transaction. The name is free again.

### 32.3 The admin section
- **People** under **Admin** in the sidebar, for the admin only (`GET /api/me`'s `admin`). The sheet: **Add someone** (their first and last name, their email and their name in the rooms, §39.6; the name is like a screen name: `^[a-z][a-z0-9_-]{0,23}$`, not reserved, not the admin's, not taken, and not held by an agent in any room or reserved by an agent that left within the last day), then **Send this to bob**: the invite text with a Copy button, shown once (the address, the email they sign in with and a one-time password good for 7 days). Then everyone with how they sign in (`password`, `passkey`, `one-time`, `expired`), **New one-time password** (a reset: their password stops working, their sessions end, their passkeys stay) and **Remove**.
- **Routes:** `GET /api/people`; `POST /api/people {name}` and `POST /api/people/{id}/password` (a fresh check, §32.4: each gives someone a way in); `POST /api/people/{id}/remove` (no check: it only takes access away). All refuse anyone but the admin (403) and a desktop broker (404). Each writes a `people` event and a notice in every room.

### 32.4 Signing in
- **Passwords.** The standard library's scrypt (n=2^14, r=8, p=1, a 16-byte salt), stored as `scrypt$…`, compared in constant time (`broker/passwords.py`). A chosen password: at least 10 characters, at most 256, more than two different characters, not the name, not shaped like a one-time password, and not in the bundled common-password blocklist (case-insensitive) or a season-plus-year pattern. The blocklist contains SHA-256 digests of 9,126 eligible entries from the NCSC 100k list; its source, checksum and Open Government Licence attribution are in `broker/common_passwords.NOTICE`. A rejection says to choose a less common password.
- **One-time passwords.** 16 Crockford base32 characters in four groups (80 bits), read back as typed (any case, with or without dashes, I and L for 1, O for 0). The admin's is the claim token (§31.3): only its SHA-256 is kept, in memory, spent by the claim, a new one every hour while unclaimed. A person's is scrypt-hashed in their row with `must_reset` and `password_expires_at` (7 days).
- **`POST /api/signin/password {name, password}`.** `name` is who signs in: an email once the admin has set one for that account, a name until then (§39). The admin is also always `admin`. The admin's one-time password starts the claim's ceremony (a cookie, as a passkey claim's) and answers `next: setup`; `/setup` then offers **Choose how you'll sign in**: `POST /api/setup/password` (the claim with a password) or `/api/setup/begin` and `/finish` (with a passkey, as in §31.3, now also without the token when the ceremony is bound). A person's one-time password gives a session whose person `must_reset`: every other route answers it 401, `/` and `/setup` serve the Choose page, and `POST /api/me/password` or adding a passkey clears it (a passkey instead clears the one-time password too). An expired one answers `expired`. Anything else wrong is "wrong email or password", after exactly one scrypt check (#179): the real hash when the name or email is someone's and they have one, the dummy hash otherwise — never both (a wrong password used to cost two, the real hash then the dummy one) and never neither (an unknown, empty, or passwordless-admin name used to cost none), so the time says nothing about who's real.
- **The limiter** (`SignInLimiter`, in memory): after 5 failures for an account in 15 minutes, that account waits 30 s, doubling to 15 minutes, before its next try (429, `slow_down`). Later failures keep the backoff until a success or 24 hours quiet. `admin`, the owner's name and email share the owner bucket; a person's name or email share `person:<id>`. Unknown names have their own name bucket. A successful password or passkey sign-in gives the browser a sealed, HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict device cookie, valid for 30 days or until the broker restarts. That browser uses its own five-failure budget instead of the account's, so strangers cannot lock it out, and one browser cannot exhaust another's budget. The cookie is no authority by itself: the right password is still required. A signed-in browser's password check has a separate budget tied to its session. Each budget has at most one check in flight (another try meanwhile is a 429), so every failure counts before that budget's next try and parallel guesses can't outrun it. scrypt runs off the event loop: strangers' checks share two workers with at most 32 running or queued, and any more are refused (429 `busy`) before any scrypt runs; a browser with a valid device cookie, and a signed-in session's check, run on a worker of their own, so a flood under made-up names doesn't hold them up. A password changed while it was being checked doesn't sign in. A success clears the budget it used.
- **Passkeys** are as in §31.4, per person: `/api/passkeys/begin` registers with the signed-in person's handle and excludes only their passkeys; `/api/passkey/finish` signs in whoever's passkey it is (a person's passkey clears a pending one-time password: it proves it's them).
- **The fresh check** (§31.4) is a passkey **or** a password: `POST /api/signin/check {password}` for a signed-in browser. The app's **Confirm it's you** dialog asks for the password (with **Use a passkey** beside it when there is one); someone with no password gets their passkey at once. It guards adding a passkey, changing a password, adding someone, a new one-time password, and pairing or approving a machine.
- **Sign out everywhere** ends that person's sessions only; `switchboard logout --all` from the container's shell still ends everyone's.
- **SSO** is a disabled button, "Coming soon" (`GET /api/auth/state`'s `sso`), until Sign in with Google is set up (§38).
- **Where passwords work:** any hosted broker, passkeys or not (an IP address or plain `http://…test` gets passwords only). Machines dial in behind any public URL that is a secure context.
- **The admin's name** is `human_name`: `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME` in the deployment sets it over `config.toml` (a container's own user would make it `me`).

### 32.5 What agents see
- **Every person is a user.** The join text says `Your users are alice and bob (kind=human): each of them is your user.` when there is more than one person (as before with one); `who()` lists them the same way; a batch's header names the person its human lines are from, or "your users" when several; the SessionStart reminder names them all. Room rule 1 is unchanged: every person's message is `kind=human`.
- **Delivery is unchanged**: a person's message is prio 2 for every recipient, resets the loop guard and wakes past an exhausted budget like the admin's. The budget and loop-guard notices name "people's messages" and "a person" once there is more than one.
- `@mentions` of people are recognized like the admin's (`mention_names`). The web UI's `@` popover offers them (§29.2, #418).

### 32.6 Threat model (a delta to §31.6)
- **Anyone added is trusted with every agent.** That is the design choice of §32.1: an invite is a key to steer everyone's agents, approvals-off ones included. Remove someone and they are out at once (their sessions end; their WebSockets close; every machine they paired or approved is removed, #178).
- **People's names cannot be agent names.** An agent join refuses a person's name and any name that starts with it; adding a person refuses an exact agent name while it is held in any room or remains in the 24-hour reuse window. This prevents an `@bob` meant for a person from marking an agent named bob as addressed.
- **A one-time password is a bearer secret until it's used**: shown once to the admin, 7 days, and useless after its person chose their own. The admin's is in the log for an hour at a time, as the claim link was.
- **Passwords can be guessed online**, which passkeys can't: the limiter slows that per account and overall, and a password needs 10 characters and cannot be on the common-password blocklist. They are never logged; only their scrypt hashes are stored.
- **A stolen session can't make itself permanent**: adding a passkey, changing the password, adding someone and making an agent token all need a check in the last five minutes, and a session on a one-time password can do nothing but choose its own.

### 32.7 Tests
- **Unit:** the v3→v4 migration on the 0.7.0 dump (`test_db_migrate_v4.py`; the older migration tests now expect the current version), passwords, a 24-hour steady-state limiter simulation and common-password checks (`test_password_guessing.py`), the one-time claim token (`test_passkey_pieces.py`), `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME` (`test_config.py`).
- **Integration** (`test_people.py`, the broker behind `https://sb.example.com` as in `test_passkeys.py`): the admin's setup with the one-time password then a password, or a passkey instead; a person's one-time password, the forced choice and everything it's refused meanwhile; everyone equal but for the admin section; an agent seeing two users and a batch from bob; an agent refused bob's name and prefix, with `@bob` not addressed to another agent; adding bob refused while an agent holds the name and for the next 24 hours; a new one-time password; removal; expiry; the limiter, including the owner's shared aliases; a passkey instead of a password; names; the fresh check; sign out everywhere per person; the desktop unchanged. `test_passkeys.py` follows the claim's new wording. `test_signin_timing.py` (#179): counting calls to `verify_password` (never timing it), exactly one real check — its `stored` argument never `None` — for a wrong and a right password against the owner with a claim open, the owner with and without a password, a one-time password, a person's own password, a passkey-only person, an unknown name and an empty one.
- **Browser** (`tests/e2e/test_people_ui.py`, `test_passkeys_ui.py`, `test_machines_ui.py`), and the image test through Caddy: the three ways in, setup, the People sheet with the invite and Copy, bob's first sign-in, Confirm it's you.

## 33. Mermaid diagrams in messages (#57)

A ```` ```mermaid ```` block in a message shows as its code, with a **Show diagram** button that draws it in place, in the page. Agents are told they may draw this way (room rule 5). Mermaid is text, a fraction of an image's tokens, and GitHub renders the same block, so a diagram works in a pull request or an issue too.

### 33.1 What you see
- The block shows its code, its language label, **Show diagram** and **Copy**. A click draws the diagram in place of the code, and the button becomes **Show code**. Copy copies the source either way.
- While a diagram is drawn, **Full screen** opens its shadow host with the Fullscreen API. The host fills the viewport on the page's light or dark background, and its SVG scales to fit. Esc or the browser's full-screen control returns to the message with the drawing intact. The button is absent when the element Fullscreen API is unavailable and hidden while code is on show. (#86)
- A diagram Mermaid can't parse keeps its code and shows Mermaid's message as text ("Can't draw this diagram: Parse error on line 3: …", cut to 400 characters), never Mermaid's error drawing.
- When the failed diagram came from an agent still in the room, its error line has **Ask <sender> to fix it**. A click posts a human reply to that message, with `@sender` and only a safe diagnosis: a parse line number and Mermaid's expected token names, the fixed text "Mermaid found no diagram type", or a fixed generic error. The source and parser snippet never go into the human's message. The person's own diagrams get no button. (#87)
- A drawing follows the person's saved theme, or the system's light or dark scheme when set to System (Mermaid's `neutral` and `dark` themes), and is redrawn when that choice or the system scheme changes.
- Nothing draws by itself: a room full of diagrams costs nothing until someone clicks (#57: render on click at first).

### 33.2 Where it runs
#57 weighed a sandboxed frame against drawing in the page. The maintainer chose the page (2026-09-30): about half the work, and the page's script rule stays as strict as before.
- **Loading.** Mermaid 11.17.2's `dist/mermaid.min.js` (3.6 MB, MIT) is vendored unmodified in `web/static/vendor/mermaid/` with its LICENSE, taken from the npm tarball after checking it against the registry's sha512. It loads on the first **Show diagram**, once: `diagram.js` creates the one script element in all static JS, for that one constant same-origin path. The bundle can't be loaded with `import()` (it is an IIFE whose last line reads a global that its own `var` shadows inside a module), and a `<script>` tag in `index.html` would make every page load download and parse it.
- **Settings.** `securityLevel: 'strict'` (labels are text, click callbacks are off, DOMPurify cleans the output), `htmlLabels: false` (top level and flowchart), journey text as SVG text (`textPlacement: 'tspan'`; its default is HTML), `maxTextSize` 20,000, `maxEdges` 500, no error drawing. Mermaid's `secure` list grows, so a diagram's own config (`%%{init}%%` or front matter) can't change the security level, the labels, the theme, colours, fonts or CSS, DOMPurify's settings or the layout engine.
- **Drawing.** Mermaid measures text in the page, and its renderers look their SVG up from `document.body`, so it can't draw inside a shadow root. `diagram.js` lets `mermaid.run` draw in an off-screen, invisible box in the page (`.md-diagram-stage`), then moves the finished `<svg>`, its `<style>` inside it, into the block's own shadow root, and removes the box. From then on its styles apply inside that shadow root only. Links in the drawing lose `href`, `xlink:href` and `target`, so md.js's `mdLink` stays the one link path (§29.4). switchboard's own code parses no HTML string: `mermaid.run` sets the box's `innerHTML` inside the vendored file.
- **Full screen.** The shadow host is the Fullscreen API element, so the drawing keeps its style boundary. The SVG exposes only a `drawing` CSS part, which lets `style.css` size it to the viewport without adding inline styles or moving it out of the shadow root. (#86)
- **One at a time.** Mermaid's config is global, so drawings are queued.
- **Fix request.** `diagram.js` displays Mermaid's cut error in the block, but dispatches only a safe diagnosis. `app.js` knows that block's message id and sender, adds the button only for current agent members, and posts through the normal queued `send` path with `reply_to`. One Service helper validates the id's SQLite range, room and chat kind for both the signed-in `/say` route and the agent `say` route before either stores a reply. (#87)

### 33.3 The CSP and the lint
- **The CSP.** Mermaid's drawings carry a `<style>` element and `style` attributes, so the signed-in app page (`GET /` when it serves `index.html`) gets `style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` (`auth.app_csp`). Everything else stays: `script-src 'self'` (no inline script, no eval), and every other response, the sign-in and setup pages included, keeps `style-src 'self'` (`auth.csp`). The maintainer's yes for this exception is quoted in #57's pull request.
- **The lint** (`tests/unit/test_web_static_lint.py`): exactly one `createElement('script')` and one `.src =` in all static JS, both in `diagram.js`, for `const SRC = '/static/vendor/mermaid/mermaid.min.js'`; the sha256 of the vendored file and of its LICENSE are pinned, and nothing else lives below `static/`, so no script escapes the lint; `diagram.js` loads before `app.js`, and the sign-in and setup pages load neither it nor Mermaid; its settings stay strict, with the secure keys.

### 33.4 Threat model
- **Diagram source is attacker-controlled message text.** A prompt-injected agent can post a hostile diagram.
- **Script.** Markup that gets past a Mermaid bug still meets `script-src 'self'`: no inline script, event handler or `javascript:` URL runs. DOMPurify cleans the output before that.
- **Styles.** A drawing's `<style>` applies inside its own shadow root, so it can't hide a ⚠, draw a fake prompt or fake a line anywhere else in the page, and `contain: paint` keeps what it paints inside its box. While Mermaid draws, its styles are in the page, off-screen, for that long; they are Mermaid's own CSS for that diagram, scoped by its id, after its sanitizing.
- **Requests and links.** `default-src`, `img-src` and `connect-src` still allow only this origin, so a drawing loads nothing from elsewhere. Links are stripped, so a click can't go to `/logout` or to another local service.
- **What remains.** A logic bug in Mermaid runs as trusted code in the page (the frame would have confined it), and `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` on the app page stays. Against that: a pinned release with every published security fix (through 11.16.1's of 2026-08-04), strict settings a diagram can't change, and drawing only on click. Updating Mermaid is a pull request that replaces the vendored file, its pinned digests and the version here (CONTRIBUTING.md).

### 33.5 Tests
- **Unit:** md.js under node: a `mermaid` block (in any case) gets the button only when `SBDiagram` is there, other languages never, and a click hands diagram.js the block, its raw source and the button. The lint above. `test_web_origin.py`: `app_csp` differs from `csp` only by `'unsafe-inline'` in `style-src`.
- **Integration:** `test_web_auth.py`: the app page's CSP, and the strict one on the sign-in page and the static scripts; `diagram.js` and the vendored file are served as plain scripts.
- **Browser** (`tests/e2e/test_web_ui.py`): a diagram draws on click and the code comes back; Mermaid loads only then, and once; no style is left in the page; Copy copies the source. Full screen uses the diagram box, and leaving it returns to the still-drawn message. Light, dark and a scheme change. A hostile diagram (an `%%{init}%%` asking for loose security, HTML labels, the forest theme, a themeCSS that hides the page and DOMPurify settings; an `<img onerror>` label; a click link to `/logout` and a click callback) draws with none of it: no HTML, no link, no dialog, and the page still signed in. It fails without the secure keys. A broken diagram keeps its code and says why; one from an agent can be sent back with a human reply and @mention in one click. A console error or a CSP violation fails each of them.

## 34. Per-person Settings (#100)

Clicking the signed-in person's name opens the shared native `<dialog>` from §29.2 as Settings, or its full-width bottom sheet on a phone. Appearance offers System, Light and Dark. Sign-in & security contains the existing password and passkey actions and sign-out actions; they work inside the dialog without submitting or closing it by accident.

**Layout and navigation (#232, closes #212).** A fixed-size sheet on a desktop (about 860 x 720, capped by the viewport) with a header that never scrolls: the title "Settings" and a × icon button (`#settings-close`). Below it, a rail on the left as a vertical WAI-ARIA tab list (`role="tablist"`, `aria-orientation="vertical"`, each tab `role="tab"` with `aria-selected` and `aria-controls`, each group's `role="tabpanel"` with `aria-labelledby`) holds five groups — **Profile**, **Appearance**, **New rooms**, **Agents** (#382, §49.1), **Sign-in & security** — with roving tabindex: Up/Down and Home/End move between tabs and select at once (automatic activation), Tab moves into the panel, Esc still closes the sheet. One group's panel shows at a time and its content scrolls, not the sheet; **Sign out** and the running version sit at the foot of the rail. Sign-in & security exists only where the Account section showed the password and passkeys before this split (a hosted broker, or `state.me.signin`): a desktop broker's rail has four tabs. On a phone (760 px and narrower) the groups show as a list instead, each row with its icon, name and a one-line summary from the current state; tapping a row opens that group on its own page, with a "‹ Settings" back button, the group's name and the × in its header, and Back returns focus to the row. Every group's panel stays in the DOM (`hidden` toggles which one shows), so a draft survives switching groups and the periodic `/api/me` refresh the sheet does while open. `openSettings(group)` takes an optional group to open at (default Profile); nothing calls it with one yet. Profile and New rooms each have a bar pinned below the scrolling content. Save and Discard start disabled; editing a field shows "Unsaved changes" and enables them, switching groups keeps each draft, Discard resets only that group's fields, and a successful Save makes it clean again. Appearance applies at once; Sign-in & security keeps its own password and passkey actions.

`preferences` is schema version 5's table: `person_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY CHECK(person_id >= 0)` and `theme TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'system' CHECK(theme IN ('system','light','dark'))`. The owner's key is 0; each other person's key is their positive `people.id`. An absent row means System, so migration v4→v5 creates the table and updates `schema_version` without rewriting people. It follows the usual checked 0600 backup and one-transaction migration. Removed people's preferences are inaccessible through authentication, like their other retained history.

`GET /api/me` returns the caller's `preferences`. `PUT /api/me/preferences` accepts a nonempty subset of `theme`, `text_size`, `room_rules`, `budget_per_hour` and `hop_limit`; the first two use fixed enums, the room rules use the cap in §35, and the last two are the room-creation defaults in §42. The server derives the person from the signed-in session. The existing host/origin and `X-Switchboard` checks apply to PUT. The index HTML sets the saved enums on `<html data-theme data-text-size>` before CSS paints, preventing a flash of the wrong theme or type size; the UI updates them after a successful save, and `style.css` handles the variants. No preference is stored in the browser. Mermaid redraws an open diagram when the theme changes.

Migration and per-person API tests use temp homes; browser tests check selection, reload, account actions, phone layout, and a diagram following the saved choice. The tabbed layout's own tests (`tests/e2e/test_web_ui.py`): the tab list by keyboard (arrows move and select, Tab enters the panel, Esc closes), the × staying visible once a long panel scrolls to its end (#212), a draft surviving a switch to another group and back and the sheet's own `/api/me` refresh, a desktop broker's four tabs, and on a phone the list's summaries, opening a group, and Back returning focus to its row.

### 34.1 Text size (#79)

Schema version 6 adds `preferences.text_size TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'default' CHECK(text_size IN ('small','default','large','larger'))`. The v5→v6 migration preserves every theme and gives existing rows Default. `GET /api/me` returns the size; a checked `PUT /api/me/preferences` can change it without replacing the theme. The Settings choices have scales .9, 1, 1.15 and 1.3. Every fixed CSS `font-size` or `font` pixel size uses `calc(<n>px * var(--text-scale))`; pixel line heights scale too. The phone composer keeps a 16 px minimum to avoid iOS focus zoom. Layout, colours and icons keep their original dimensions, with room to grow where text wraps.

The signed-in app and the password-reset setup page set `data-text-size` in the broker's HTML response, before paint. The sign-in and initial claim pages are anonymous and use Default: the broker has no person's preference to load before authentication, and it neither exposes a person's size by name nor stores it in a browser cookie or local storage. They share the scalable stylesheet. The browser test measures the log, sidebar, composer, Members and Inspector at Larger, checks reload and phone width, and the CSS test rejects a bare fixed font size.

## 35. Custom room rules (#105)

The optional `preferences.room_rules` is per person. A new room copies its creator's current text to `rooms.rules_text`; later edits to either record do not change the other. Schema v7 adds both checked columns with empty defaults, a 2,000-character cap, and a v6→v7 migration after the usual verified backup. Old rooms and people start with no custom rules. Budgets and hop limits remain separate room settings, each with the same copy-on-create model (§42).

Only an authenticated person may write defaults through the Origin- and `X-Switchboard`-checked `PUT /api/me/preferences`, or room text through `PUT /api/rooms/{slug}/rules`. The room's header opens the shared native dialog to edit it; Settings has the defaults editor. On a phone both dialogs rise from the bottom. The human-only `room.rules` RPC backs `switchboard rooms rules ROOM [--set TEXT]`; an MCP member has no such role. A `say()` or room command cannot change rules. A successful room edit writes a notice naming the person and refreshes browser room listings.

`render_join` puts the room's current rules after the seven fixed rules, under a heading that says the fixed rules win any conflict. The text is `envelope.sanitize`d without truncation; both join and delivery frame the whole capped value. Schema v8 adds `rooms.rules_version` and `memberships.rules_seen`; migration starts existing rooms at version 1 and existing members at seen version 0, so they receive current rules once. A join marks its member as having seen that version. An edit to different text increments the version. The delivery engine includes rules only while a member is behind, checks that the whole frame fits, and advances `rules_seen` only when the frame is returned or a push succeeds. If the rules cannot fit, it sends the batch without them and keeps the member behind for a later batch. The browser receives rules as text only. Tests cover the migrations, copied defaults, cap and checked routes, human-only RPC role, fixed-rule ordering and hygiene, once-only delivery, deferred oversized rules, and the desktop and phone editors.

## 36. Report-only security scans (#101)

The security workflow runs on code pull requests and on code merges to `main`, independently of the required `CI` aggregate in `test.yml`. It uses the same docs-only and release skip logic as that workflow. Each scanner has read-only repository permission and keeps its output as an Actions artifact for 14 days. Findings do not fail a pull request, and no SARIF is uploaded to code scanning. A scanner error remains visible in its job log, but the report-only job cannot block a merge. No repository setting or required check changes.

CodeQL runs the `security-extended` queries for Python, JavaScript/TypeScript and Actions, with SARIF saved locally and the vendored Mermaid bundle excluded from source analysis. Bandit reports Python patterns. pip-audit checks the `uv.lock` export, including development packages; OSV checks the Mermaid version named by the pinned vendor comment. Trivy scans the image built from that commit. zizmor scans workflows. gitleaks scans the full Git history, redacting any discovered secrets before it saves the report. The local `scripts/security_scan.py` command runs the fast Bandit, dependency and workflow layers. There is no finding baseline or severity gate in this first step; contributors inspect the artifacts and fix or explain findings in later pull requests.

## 37. A review board in a room (#80)

### 37.1 What it is, and what switchboard never does
Agents reviewing one pull request together used to settle it in chat, and by the end nobody could say which findings were open, who owned each one, or what was about to be posted (#80). A **board** holds that state instead: findings and questions, each with a state and an owner, worked by the agents through one tool and decided by a person where they can't agree. The agents do 90% of the triage; the person does the last 10%: the questions, the contested findings, and a spot-check of the evidence.

switchboard never fetches the pull request, judges a finding, runs a model, holds a GitHub or GitLab token, or posts, comments, pushes, approves or merges anything. The one agent chosen at handoff posts the review in its own name, with its own `gh` or `glab` (§37.6). A move is a tool call, never parsed out of prose.

### 37.2 Items and moves (`switchboard/reviews.py`, pure)
- A **finding** is `raised` by a member. The other side **concedes** it (naming an `owner`, an agent in the room, by default itself) or **contests** it (with a reason); a contest goes back to its raiser, who **drops** the finding or **insists** on it (§37.13). A conceded finding is **fixed** by its owner (with the commit and a test name or http(s) proof link), then **confirmed** by its independent raiser (#251). Whoever raised a finding can't concede or contest it, only **drop** it (with a reason), or **insist** on it once it is contested: settling it is the other side's call.
- A **question** is **asked** by a member: a title, 2 to 4 options, and the one it recommends, which is required (#423: a question with options carries a recommendation). A finding may offer 2 to 4 **fix options** in the same shape, with its raiser's recommendation. The other agents **concur** with the recommendation or pick another option, and an option reads "recommended" only when every agent has picked it (§37.12). Only a person answers or drops a question.
- A person may also rule on a contested finding (concede it, with an owner, or drop it) and drop anything. Those moves come with the web UI (§37.5).
- **Outcomes that couldn't be recorded (#371).** A conceded finding moves to another owner with another `concede`: its owner hands it over, and once the owner has left the room (an agent restarted under a new name: the finding was otherwise no one's to fix) any agent but its raiser takes it over (`agent_move(..., owner_here=False)`). A fix from another agent while the owner is gone is told to take it over first. A person reassigns any conceded finding (`concede` again, with a new owner), rules one fixed (`fix`: settled at once, `confirmed_by` the person, on their say), and confirms any fix, the raiser's check or not. The web UI's owner picker starts on an agent other than the raiser, which it used to preselect when the raiser joined first. The event of a reassignment says `reassigned`. **Deferred to a ticket:** `defer(item, reason=<ticket>)` settles an open finding for this pull request, to be done in a follow-up ticket: by its raiser (the call is the raiser's, like a drop) or a person (the page's **Defer**, with the ticket as the `reason`, since the route takes only the fields it lists). It is stored as `dropped` with `deferred_to` set (schema 23), so everything that settles a drop settles it, and it reads "deferred to <ticket>" in `show`, "Deferred to <ticket>" on the page (not struck out) and "Deferred: <title> — to <ticket>" in the handoff summary. The tool's description names it, within the 3,700-character list: it now opens "One pull request's review board" and no longer ends "Returns the board.".
- **Settled:** every finding dropped or fixed and confirmed, and no question open. A new fixed finding stays open until confirmation; old fixed rows remain settled through migration.
- Labels are per board and per kind: F1, F2, … and Q1, … . Input is checked, not cut: a title over 200 characters, a URL that isn't `http(s)`, a `head` that isn't 7 to 64 hex digits, `lines` that aren't `42` or `40-58`, or options that repeat are refused with what to fix. A board holds at most 200 items.

### 37.3 Storage (schema version 9; `deferred_to` since 23; `review_picks` since 26; `insisted` since 27)
`reviews(id, room_id, url, head, opened_by, opened_at, closed_at, posted_at, posted_by)` and `review_items(id, review_id, kind, n, state, title, detail, file, lines, raised_by, owner, commit_sha, reason, options, recommend, answer, answered_by, created_at, updated_at, proof, confirmed_by)`, with each length a CHECK too. A room's boards are the newest review of each URL, open or closed (§37.14). The v8→v9 migration only creates the two tables, after the usual checked backup (§27.6); a test pins that a fresh database and a migrated one have the same tables. Every move also records a `review` event. At handoff, `posted_at` records when the person sent the plan and `posted_by` names the chosen agent; these existing columns need no migration (#252). `review_items.insisted` (§37.13) and `review_picks(item_id, agent, choice, picked_at)`, primary key `(item_id, agent)`, holds the picks agents make with `concur` (§37.12); the v25→v26 migration only creates it, empty, after the usual checked backup. The asker's own pick stays in `review_items.recommend`, so nothing is rewritten and every question asked before 26 keeps its recommendation as its asker's pick. Schema 28 adds `reviews.closed_by`, `reopened_by` and `reopened_at` and stamps every stored `review` event with its board (§37.14).

### 37.4 The tool
`review(room, action, …)` (§6.1): `open`, `list`, `show`, `raise`, `ask`, `concur`, `concede`, `contest`, `insist`, `fix`, `confirm`, `drop`, `defer`, `report`, `close`, `reset`. It goes through `agent.review`, a member method like `agent.say`, so only a member of the room can call it, with its credential. Every item move returns the board as text, open work first, one line per item, so an agent always acts on the current state. `open` with the board's own URL and a new head moves the board to that commit. A room holds several boards, one per URL, and a closed one is kept (§37.14); `url` says which board a call is about.

`close(reason=...)` closes a settled board at any time, including after handoff, with no reason required. An unsettled board needs a reason of at most 500 characters; an empty or oversized one is refused before state changes. One room notice reads `Review board for #7 closed by claude-1: moving on. Open items: F1, Q1.` for a GitHub PR, listing every unsettled label (including a fix awaiting confirmation); GitLab uses `!7`, and an unrecognized forge uses the checked URL. On a settled board with no reason it ends `closed by claude-1.`. The reason and labels are recorded with the close event, the board row and items remain stored, and the hub publishes the room's remaining open boards (none, once the last closes) so the page drops it. This is a member action through the tool, never an instruction taken from another agent's chat. Since #445 the board is kept: `open` (or a person's `/review`) brings it back with its items and history, and only its holder or a person closes it (§37.14). This implements #408 as accepted by the maintainer ("Proceed", 2026-10-18).

A new `raise` call gives its location as `where="shop/cart.py:40-58"`; a path alone or `:42` for a line without a path is also allowed. The broker splits it at the final colon and applies the existing file length and `reviews.lines` range checks before storing separate `file` and `lines` values. It accepts the older `file` and `lines` broker fields for one release so a running older MCP server can still raise findings. A call mixing `where` with either older field is refused. The MCP tool schema advertises only `where`. A size test caps the compact names, descriptions and input schemas of the whole MCP tool list at 3,500 characters. #274 set it at 3,000, down from 3,990; `status`, `resume`, `since`, `before`, `proof` and `confirm` then each had to fit by cutting other tools' descriptions, down to "Away; omit to clear." and "Members/status/tier." The maintainer raised it to 3,500 so every description says what its tool does; the list measures 3,444. A new tool or parameter still has to fit, and shortens nothing below a full sentence to do it.

A move that hands the next step to another agent is that agent's to hear (`reviews.handoff`, pure): a raised finding is the other agents' to concede or contest, a finding conceded to another agent is that owner's to fix, a fix is its raiser's to check, a contested finding is its raiser's to drop or insist on (#424, §37.13), and a question just asked is the other agents' to agree with or answer with another pick (#423, §37.12). Such a move is posted as the mover's own chat message with switchboard's words and the item's title, @mentioning whoever acts next ("Review board: F1 raised: discount applied after tax. @claude-1: concede or contest it. review(action="show") shows the board."), so it wakes them as a `say` would, under the room's wake budget and loop guard. Any other move (a concur, an insist, a drop, a concession that leaves the fix with the conceder, a fix with no other raiser, a contest whose raiser has left) is a **notice** ("claude-1 conceded F1, owner claude-1"): it isn't delivered, wakes no one and costs no agent a turn. A finding both sides have argued (insisted on), and a question once the agents have had their say, are a person's, who sees them on the page; a person's own decisions are already their messages (§37.5). Until a recorded review found it (2026-10-11), every move was a notice, so an agent listening in `wait()` never heard a finding raised against it, and a review that stayed on the board stood still. The tool isn't rate-limited like `say`: the board caps its own size.

`agent.review` is a member method, so an agent on another machine moves its own cards like any other: scoped to the rooms that agent joined, and moves only that room's board. Devin's install pre-approves `review` with the other eight tools. Both widened with the maintainer's yes (2026-10-05: "Each agent should be able to move their kanban cards"; "Can we fix this? That's a problem").

### 37.5 The page
- **Where:** a header button, shown only when the room has a board, swaps the log for the board (`#main.board-open`); the composer stays. Three lanes in one row, read as the life of a finding (#250): **Agents working** (raised, conceded, contested, and fixed awaiting confirmation), **Needs you** (open questions, findings both sides have argued, and fixes needing a person) and **Settled** (confirmed fixes, answered questions, and dropped findings, last and muted with their reason). No lane wraps under another on a desktop; on a phone they stack with Needs you first. A card whose next move is one agent's (`boardNext`: a conceded finding's owner, an unconfirmed fix's raiser, a contested finding's raiser (#424); never a raised finding, which any other agent answers, or one that needs a person) carries that member's status from the room's members frame (`boardPulse`, #407): the Members dot's colors, pulsing and with the word "working" while it's busy, its accessible name saying whose and that busy means "on this or something else", since switchboard can't tell which. An agent whose person has a permission prompt open (`waiting-approval`, §49.9, #472) gets an amber ring and the words "waiting for approval" instead, and its name says so (it is not working, and it answers nobody until they act). The card's top row wraps instead of squeezing, so in a narrow lane (about 110 px, a 1280 px window) the words go on a second row and the card's label stays on one line. `prefers-reduced-motion` stops the pulse; the word stays. Since every members frame rebuilds the board, a card with keyboard focus keeps it through the rebuild, as the divider, Hand off and Close board do (#430: Close board was the one control in the header that did not, and the next Tab after a redraw started from the top of the page). A card opens its detail beside the lanes, at a share of the board's width (`min(45%, 560px)`, never under 340 px), with a divider between the two (#343): a focusable `role=separator` that is dragged, or moved with the arrow keys, Home and End, to trade width between the card and the lanes, which never go under 420 px. The page's script writes no styles, so the width is one of a ladder of classes on `.board-body` (`dw-0`, 340 px, to `dw-60` in 24 px steps, and `dw-max`), kept in the page's state so a redraw and the next card open at it; it is not stored, and a reload starts over. Under 778 px of board (a container query on the board, so opening Members counts) the card goes below the lanes and there is no divider; a contested finding's two sides stack inside a card under 440 px. The detail is short and linking out: the title and the evidence through md.js; **Lines**, a link to those lines with the forge's mark (`reviews.lines_url`). On GitHub it is the PR's own diff, anchored at `diff-<sha256 of the path>R<a>-R<b>`, labelled "View shop/cart.py:40-58 on GitHub". On GitLab (#442) it is the file at the board's head commit, `{base}/-/blob/<head>/<path>#L40-58` (`#L40` for one line), which GitLab highlights exactly, labelled "lines 40–58 at abc1234" ("line 40", or "file" when the finding has no lines), with the path as its tooltip; and a second link, `reviews.diff_url`, to the file in the MR diff, `{base}/-/merge_requests/<n>/diffs#<sha1 of the path>`, labelled "file in the MR diff". An MR diff's line anchors are line codes (`<sha1>_<old>_<new>`) that need the old side's position, which a board doesn't have, and it has no range anchor, so the diff link opens the file and says so. A GitLab finding on a board with no head, or whose path is not a plain relative one, has no blob link and keeps the diff link. Or the path as text with no forge; **Proof**, the test name or checked http(s) link; **Fix**, the commit (`reviews.commit_url`: in the PR on GitHub, the commit's page on GitLab); for a contested finding, both sides side by side (the raiser's evidence, and the contest's reason under whoever contested it); a short history, who raised or asked it and each move since, from the board's `review` events (`reviews.history`, at most 12 lines); and the person's moves. Every link goes through `externalLink`, and is built only from the forge's parts, hex digests, a checked commit and checked line numbers, and, in a GitLab blob link, the finding's path percent-encoded a segment at a time and refused (no link) when it is empty, absolute, has a doubled slash, a `.` or `..` segment, a backslash or a control character, so nothing else an agent wrote is in one. The forge's data carries `base` (the repository's page) for them.
- **The check** (#427). A card whose fix is confirmed (`state === 'fixed'` with a `confirmed_by`: its raiser's check, or a person's, which is the Settled lane's own test) has the page's `check` icon at the end of its top row, in `--green`, the theme's success colour, in light and dark. The icon is decorative: `icon()` sets `aria-hidden`, and the card already says "Fixed" and "confirmed by …" in words. A fix still waiting for its raiser's confirmation, and every other state (raised, being fixed, contested, dropped or deferred, a question), has none, so the mark means done. A dropped finding's muted, struck-through style is unchanged. The page draws the card from the board's items on every `review` frame, so a confirmation adds the check where the card stands.
- **The header** (#247) names the pull request, and since #425 carries Hand off (§37.6). `reviews.forge` (pure) reads the board's URL by its shape alone, never fetching it: a `github.com/<owner>/<repo>/pull/<n>` is GitHub's, and a `…/<group>/<project>/-/merge_requests/<n>` on any host is GitLab's (the `/-/` segment is GitLab's own, so a self-hosted GitLab and subgroups count). The board's data carries it as `forge` (`kind`, `repo`, `number`), or `null`. A recognized one shows as the forge's mark (Simple Icons' path, CC0, drawn with `createElementNS` like the icons), the repository, and `#7` or `!7`, inside the same checked link, which also gets an `aria-label`; anything else shows the URL, with no mark, since a wrong mark would say the link goes somewhere it doesn't. A name that is percent-encoded or only dots, a URL with a user name or password, or a port that isn't a number isn't recognized. The short commit after it is 7 characters, with the full one as its title.
- **Who can use it** (#249, #299; retired in 0.38.0, #385). A session's MCP server used to claim its version and tools in `mcp.hello`, and the board named a member that couldn't use it (one behind this broker, or whose tools had no `review`), with a room note suggesting `switchboard update` on a join. A session over HTTPS has the tools of the server it talks to, so every member can use the board; the claims, `board_why`, `behind_by_minor` and the note are gone, and so is the `version`/`tools` pair in Members and `who`.
- **Data:** `GET /api/rooms/{slug}/review` (any signed-in session) gives the room's open boards, `{"boards": [...]}`, the one opened first first; the web subscriber takes the hub's `review` frames, which every move publishes, each holding every open board of the room (an empty list once the last closes). With more than one board the page shows tabs (§37.14). Every string on the page is an agent's: text through `textContent`, the detail through md.js, the pull request's URL through `externalLink`, the one link path (§29.4).
- **The card's detail and its options (#423, §37.12).** A card's detail goes through `mdBody`, the chat's own Markdown path (`md.js`), so a heading, a list, code, a table and a link read as they do in a message, and a ```` ```mermaid ```` block has **Show diagram** and draws as §33 describes, with the same strict settings, the same shadow root and the same CSP: nothing here runs HTML. A card is kept across the board's rebuilds while nothing it shows changed (every members frame rebuilds the board, §37.5 above), so a drawn diagram stays drawn and a reason being typed is not lost. A question's options are buttons that each carry their own text, never a letter or a number as a label. The recommended one is listed first and says who is behind it, in the broker's words (`consensus.notes`): "(claude-1 recommends this)", "(recommended by claude-1 and codex-1)", or, in a split, "(picked by codex-1)" on each side. Only an agreed option is in the primary colour. A line under the options says who is still to pick, or that the agents are split. A finding's fix options are a list with the same notes.
- **A person's moves:** `POST /api/rooms/{slug}/review` with `action` (`answer`, `concede` (a ruling, or a reassignment of a conceded finding), `confirm`, `fix` (#371), `drop`, `handoff`, `close`), `item`, and `option`, `owner`, `reason` or the handoff `agent`; a signed-in person with the `X-Switchboard` header, like `say` (§29). `Boards.person` checks the move (including the confirmation fallback; `reviews.person_move` handles the older moves) and stores it. **Each decision is posted as that person's own message** ("Review board: Q1 answered with option 0."), through `human_say`, so it reaches the agents the way anything a person says does, with their name on it. Close is a notice, as an agent's close now is, and a person may close any board, whoever opened it. Every move names its board by `url` (§37.14). **That message carries no agent's text** (#177): only the label, the option's number, an owner checked against the room's members and the person's own reason. An agent's title or option inside a person's message would reach every agent as that person's words (`kind=human`), @mentions included; the agents read those from the board, as other agents' text. Person moves mark their review events so the handoff can find their decisions on lines.
- **Tests:** `test_review_board.py` (the routes: the person's answer and ruling as their messages that the agents read, close and reopen, sign-in and the write header, unknown fields, the `forge` field); `test_reviews.py` (`forge` on GitHub, gitlab.com, a self-hosted GitLab, subgroups, and the URLs it refuses; the Lines and Fix links for each forge and for none, GitLab's blob link with its encoded path and every path it refuses, and its diff link (#442); an item's history from events); `test_review_board.py`'s links and history from a real board; `test_board_tools.py` (a current member, one from before the list, a list without `review`, a reconnect.); `test_mcp_claims.py` (the claims' checks, and the hello's list equal to the registered tools); the e2e board tests (the three lanes in one row and stacked on a phone; a card's Lines and Fix links and history; a contested finding's two sides; a member that can't use the board, named the moment it joins; the check on a confirmed fix's card and no other, in light, dark and on a phone, and under node in `test_web_app_behavior.py` for every state (#427); the header for each forge and for neither, on a phone too; lanes, the board button, an answer and a ruling moving their cards at once and showing in the log, a drop that needs a reason, close behind a confirm, the phone in light and dark with no sideways scroll).

### 37.6 One handoff to the PR or MR (#252)
- **The plan** (`reviews.handoff_plan`, pure): one summary of every settled finding and question, including dropped items, and one line comment per finding with a recorded person decision and a file/line. The comment targets the end of a checked line range. A person `concede`, `drop` or `confirm` event carries `person: true`; an agent's event cannot create a person-decided line. For boards opened before #252, whose events lack the marker, a decision actor who never held an agent membership in that room is treated as a person; past membership names are retained for this check. The board carries the exact summary and comment text for the dialog and the chosen agent's `review(action="show")` result. Agent-written titles, options, reasons and proof stay in that board/tool data.
- **The page:** **Hand off** is in the board's header, beside the title and the status, and in no lane (#425; it was a card at the top of Settled, which read as one of the settled findings, though it acts on the whole board; the maintainer chose the header: "I'll take it", 2026-10-19). It has three states, each a line (`#board-handoff-status`) and, until the handoff, the button (`#board-handoff`): **blocked**, with the button disabled until the board has items, every fix is confirmed, every question is answered or dropped, and one of the clicking person's agents is in the room, and the line saying what is left in plain words (`boardLeft`, from the board's items, most-yours first, each kind with its own count: "1 question to answer", "1 finding to decide" for a contested one, "2 fixes to confirm", "3 findings in progress" for a raised or conceded one; "No items yet" for an empty board, "None of your agents is in this room" for a settled one with none of theirs); **ready**, the button enabled and the line "5 of 5 settled"; and **done**, the line "Handed off by claude-1 at 09:40" (with the day, "on Sat, Oct 17 at 09:40", when it wasn't today) in place of the button. The line is the button's `aria-describedby`. The status chip is unchanged. When the last item settles the button gets the `nudge` class, a halo that grows and fades once (`board-handoff-nudge`, 1.8 s): no toast, no sound, and none for a board that is already ready when it opens (a closed board forgets whether it was ready). A person's own move redraws the board twice, from the reply and from the live frame, so the class lasts the animation's length (`BOARD_NUDGE_MS`) instead of one render; `prefers-reduced-motion` drops every animation (one rule in style.css), and the enabled button is the cue. Every redraw rebuilds the button, so a focused one gets its focus back, and when a move has left it disabled, or the handoff has replaced it, the line beside it takes the focus instead of the page losing it. On a phone the header is sticky: the lanes stack, a card's detail is below them, and a person answers the last question down there, so the header, and Hand off with its highlight, stays on screen. Its dialog previews the exact summary and line comments and offers **Posted by** from the clicking person's active room agents; the opener is the default when it belongs to them and is still present, otherwise their first active agent. The broker maps each active member's host to its paired person (§44.3), and checks the choice again when the form is submitted. Escape or Cancel returns focus to Hand off. After handoff the status names the agent and focus moves to the handoff result.
- **The move:** `POST /api/rooms/{slug}/review` with `action: "handoff", agent`, refused unless the board is settled and has items, the chosen agent belongs to the clicking person and is an active member, and handoff has not occurred. The existing `posted_at`/`posted_by` fields record the request time and chosen agent; later item moves are refused so the plan does not change, while reports and closure remain available. It sends **one message from the person** (`reviews.render_handoff`) to that agent only. The message has checked labels and an instruction to read the exact plan with `review(action="show")`, post one review through its own `gh` or `glab`, and leave line conversations open. No agent-written text or URL is repeated as the person's words (#177); an overlong message is refused before state changes.
- **What switchboard does:** stores the handoff and sends that one message. **What it doesn't:** fetch the PR or MR, hold a token, post, comment, approve or merge anything (§37.1).
- **Tests:** `handoff_plan` and `render_handoff`, including the person decision marker, message hygiene and size bound (unit); the header's words for every blocked case with their singular and plural, ready, done, and the nudge's window, in node (`test_web_app_behavior.py`, scenario `board_handoff_header`); refusal before confirmed fixes, selected-agent ownership, one message and one handoff (integration); the three header states in light, dark and on a phone with the words updating as agents settle items and no Hand off in any lane, Tab order and focus kept through redraws, no highlight for a board that opens ready, disabled button, dialog preview and agent choice, Escape focus, and phone layout (e2e); and Close board keeping its focus through a members frame and a review frame, and Shift+Tab from it going back to the header's link, not the top of the page (e2e, #430).

### 37.8 A person starts a review: `/review` (#248)
- **The command:** `/review <url> [note]` (§10), `human_cli` like `/catchup`, since all it does is open a board and post a message as the person.
