It is behind the agent token on purpose. `/healthz` answers anyone, with any Host, and stays exactly `ok` (§30.3), so the version is never a fingerprint for someone with no token. The join over HTTPS says it too (§49.5).

### 49.3 Who is calling
- **The device** is the token's, and it is the host of its agents' sessions, as a machine's was: a Codex thread's key is `codex@<device>:<thread id>`. No host view knows it (every probe answers "can't tell", never "dead"), and `host_person` answers with the token's person, ahead of any machine of that name (§44.3).
- **The harness** is the client's claim, from its `clientInfo`: `codex-mcp-client` is Codex, `claude-code` Claude Code. Any other client is refused (`unsupported`), because its calls must name their session, and only these two can: Codex puts its thread on each call (`_meta.threadId`), and Claude Code's PreToolUse hook says which session makes each call (below).
- **Claude Code's session.** Its calls name the call (`_meta["claudecode/toolUseId"]`), never the session. A PreToolUse hook, an `mcp_tool` hook on switchboard's own tools, calls the hidden `hook` tool just before each call with `session_id`, `tool_use_id` and `tool_name` (its input template; nothing else of the session is sent). `HookUses` keeps each report under its token for two minutes (at most 4,096), so one device's reports never name another's sessions, and only PreToolUse counts. A call takes its session from the report, waiting up to `HOOK_WAIT_S` (2 s) for one that hasn't arrived (in the spike it came 5 ms ahead). With none, the call is refused and says to set the hook up. The session is then `claude@<device>:<session id>`, with the `claude@https` adapter (`adapters/https.py`, `HttpsClaudeAdapter`): pull only, tier `claude:https`. The engine picks a harness's `@https` adapter for a session over HTTPS (`over_https`: MCP pid 0 on a device's host), and Codex, which has none yet, keeps `codex@remote`.
- **The hook tool** is left out of `tools/list` (`HideHook`), so the model never sees it, and a hook calls it anyway (both harnesses did in the spike). It answers `{}`: a hook reply that decides nothing.
- **The session.** A fresh `HttpConn` per call stands in for a connection: its host is the device, and nothing about it is a process (there is no pid to probe). `AgentService.http_session` finds the caller's participant by its key on the device.
- **No credentials.** A session's memberships are read from the store on every call (`http_rooms`, and `http_room`, which takes a renamed room's old name too, #316). A call names its membership as `m:<id>`, and `_http_member` accepts it only if it is the caller's own session's and active, then checks the rest as for a credential (the adapter's `credential_error`, `before_call`). So neither a broker restart nor a new MCP session drops anyone.
- **Delivery.** Codex on a device gets the `codex@https` adapter and Claude Code the `claude@https` one (`adapters/https.py`, §49.4): woken at stop by their own Stop hooks, and between turns by their open `wait()` and their `read()` and `say()` answers.
- **Liveness.** There is no process to probe: a member over HTTPS has the status its hooks report (§49.4), plus Codex's normal MCP DELETE as a SessionEnd signal. A forced kill sends no DELETE; closing an SSE/TCP connection alone is not treated as an end, since a transport can reconnect. The member keeps its memberships while offline, and leaves only when it chooses to or its token is revoked, which ends its device's members at once ("left (<device> token revoked)"). Removing a person does the same for each of their devices.
- **Tests.** `tests/integration/test_agents_http_claude.py` drives it with what Claude Code 2.1.300 sends: two sessions on one device as two members, a call that comes before its hook's report, a call with none, another token's report, a hook event other than PreToolUse, and the hook tool hidden and answering `{}`. `tests/integration/test_agents_http.py` drives `/mcp` with what Codex 0.158.0 sends (`tests/fixtures/mcp_http/`, recorded in the spike): two threads as two members, say, read, pass, leave and `wait()`; a new MCP session keeping the memberships; every tool served; no token, a bad one, a browser, another token's session; a revoked token; a call without its thread; a client that isn't Codex; a membership named by another session; a token's person and a private room; last use, and the member's version and tools.

### 49.4 Waking, and status from hooks (#383)
No push path reaches the machine an agent over HTTPS runs on, so an idle one is woken by its own listener hook, held open on the broker until a message comes: a park, as Cursor's stop hook has (§9.4), with the same engine (`park_s`, `open_park`, `_park`, the follow-up confirmed by `continue_verdict`). Codex listens at Stop; Claude Code listens at Stop and after startup or resume.
- **Hooks** are `mcp_tool` hooks calling the hidden `hook` tool, each with its own input template (Codex fails a hook whose template names a field its event lacks). `hook_params` turns what a template filled in into the params of §7.2, cut and checked; values may come as text, booleans or JSON. `AgentService.http_hook` takes the session the hook names on the token's device (no process to walk), then does what §7.4 says: status (busy at UserPromptSubmit and PostToolUse, idle at Stop, offline at SessionEnd, and `waiting-approval` between a permission prompt's Notification and the next hook, §49.9), the approval mode from `permission_mode` (both adapters report it, so Members shows it), confirmations, and context or a park. A hook of a session that hasn't joined does nothing and answers `{}`.
- **SessionStart and SessionEnd for Claude Code** go another way: Claude Code runs no `mcp_tool` hook at either (2.1.283 and 2.1.299: "no MCP client context", #399). The plugin runs `bin/session`, a command hook (POSIX `sh` and `curl`, the token on stdin; on Windows the PowerShell script `bin/windows/session.ps1`, §49.5), which posts a small JSON object to `/mcp/session` (`SessionHook`, in `AGENT_PATHS`): the event, the session id, and `source`, `reason` or `permission_mode` when they are plain words, nothing else. The route takes those two events only, so it can't park or wake a session, and handles each exactly as the hook tool does: a SessionEnd takes the session offline, and a SessionStart may answer with context in the checked shape, which the script prints for Claude Code. It always exits 0 within 5 s.
- **What a hook sends.** Its template's fields. Before a call, for switchboard's own tools only (PreToolUse says which session makes the call). After a call there are two reports (#422), because a busy agent hears a priority message only through a hook, and an agent that does only Bash calls no switchboard tool:
  - **A switchboard tool's own** (PostToolUse, and Claude Code's PostToolUseFailure) includes the tool's result, which is switchboard's own text: its batch tokens confirm a `read()`, `wait()` or `say()` answer.
  - **Every other tool's** carries no result at all: the event, the session, the call's id and the tool's name (Codex also the turn). Never the tool's input or result (a Bash output, a file, a secret), the working directory or the transcript's path. Switchboard's reply is the same as for any PostToolUse (the pending priority messages as context, or `{}`), so the hook's cost is one small request per call, which the server answers with `{}` and writes nothing for when the session is in no room. It has a 2 s timeout: a server that refuses the connection fails the hook at once and the call goes on (Claude Code 2.1.304 and Codex 0.158.0, live), and one that never answers holds the call for 2 s. The broker acknowledges a context reply when it writes it (#380's decision 5), so a reply that arrives after the timeout is a message the agent never saw; it stays in the room's history. A slow link makes that likelier at 2 s than at the 10 s of the hooks for switchboard's own tools.
  - **Not twice for switchboard's tools.** Claude Code's group for other tools has the matcher `^(?!mcp__plugin_switchboard_switchboard__)`, a negative look-ahead that Claude Code 2.1.304 takes (live: no second report for a switchboard call). Codex's matchers are Rust regular expressions, which have no look-around (0.158.0 skips such a hook with a warning), so its group matches every tool, switchboard's included. `second_report` drops that duplicate on the server: a PostToolUse of a switchboard tool, by either harness's name for it (`mcp__switchboard__…` or `mcp__plugin_switchboard_switchboard__…`), sent with no `tool_response`; the report with the result does everything it would. `tool_response` is `None` only when the hook sent none, so an empty result is still a result.
  - No prompt text is ever sent: a wake is confirmed by the session's next hook instead (below).
- **A subagent's tool calls (#446).** Claude Code runs the session's hooks for a subagent's tool calls too, with the session's own `session_id`. So a subagent looked like the session: it took the session's messages as hook context (they counted as delivered, so the session got them late, once, at its next Stop, or never), and each of its calls reported the session busy and ended the listener of an idle one, which left the session deaf until its person typed. Claude Code 2.1.305 puts an `agent_id` and an `agent_type` in a subagent's hook input (PreToolUse, PostToolUse, SubagentStart and SubagentStop) and neither in the session's own calls; a session started with `claude --agent` has an `agent_type` on its own calls but no `agent_id`, so only `agent_id` says it is a subagent (recorded live: `tests/fixtures/payloads/claude/*_subagent.json` and `PostToolUse_bash_agent_flag.json`). The plugin's PreToolUse, PostToolUse and PostToolUseFailure hooks send `"agent_id": "${agent_id}"`, which Claude Code fills with an empty string for the session's own calls. The hook tool answers `{}` to any hook with an `agent_id` that says something (`from_subagent`, whatever its length or type: a value that isn't recognized is a subagent's, never the session's own) without touching the session: no context, no confirmation (a batch token in a subagent's own `read()` result stays unconfirmed, so that answer is offered again), no status, no end to a listener. The messages wait for the session's own next hook or, for an idle session, its listener. PreToolUse is the one exception: its report only says which session makes a call, so a subagent can still call the session's switchboard tools, as it always could, and the call is the session's. A plugin that sends `agent_id` needs a server that takes it: the hook tool refuses a field it doesn't know, so update the server first. **Codex** (0.158.0, live) runs the same hooks for a subagent that `spawn_agent` starts: the parent's `session_id`, the subagent's own `turn_id` (a new one for each of its turns, and no SubagentStart for a follow-up turn), and `agent_id` and `agent_type` on the subagent's calls only (`tests/fixtures/payloads/codex/PostToolUse_bash_subagent.json`). Its plugin can't send `agent_id` the way Claude Code's does, because Codex fails a hook whose template names a field the call lacks ("hook input placeholder … was not found"), and the session's own calls lack it. Codex instead compares a tool hook's `turn_id` with the session's current turn: a different one gets `{}` before it can confirm a batch, change status or take context. The first prompt can precede `join`, when there is no member to record it on; the first PostToolUse of one of the session's switchboard tools then anchors that turn. A Stop-block wake keeps the same turn id through its next tool hooks (0.158.0, live), so they still receive context. If a session has no known turn, its tool hooks remain eligible; nothing in their template identifies a subagent until a parent turn is known.
- **Replies** are written by the broker (#380's decision 5) and checked against exact shapes (`hook_replies.reply_for`, `_shape_ok`): the context shape of §7.3 (`render`, so the same events and the same size limits), and for Codex's Stop only, `{"decision": "block", "reason": <text>}`. Anything else answers `{}`. A reply that carries a batch is acknowledged when it is delivered (`hook_ack`).
- **Codex** (`codex@https`, `HttpsCodexAdapter`) is woken in listening mode (#380's decision 2). Its Stop hook's template adds `"wait_s": "<seconds>"`, a number the hook config sets just under its own `timeout`. A Stop with `wait_s` is held that long, less 5 s, and at most `LISTEN_S` (a day; the plugin asks for just under it); without it, a Stop only reports idle. The hook's own `timeout` is not what keeps the call open: the MCP server's `tool_timeout_sec` is (#449). Codex 0.158.0, live against a throwaway broker, directly and behind Caddy: with the setting absent it ended the Stop hook's call after 5 min 2 s to 5 min 7 s ("Hook failed", `tool call failed for switchboard/hook`); set to 2 it ended it within seconds; set to 86400 the call stayed open for over ten minutes (621 s) and an @mention then woke the session, which replied. So the HTTPS server entry in USAGE and in Settings → Agents sets `tool_timeout_sec = 86400`, longer than the plugin's `wait_s` of 86390. A proxy is not the cap: the broker's answer stream carries a keepalive every 15 s, and the run behind Caddy failed at the same five minutes. A Codex thread idle for longer than a day, or across a broker restart, waits for its person to type. A message for it answers the hook with `decision: block`, and Codex carries on with it; its next hook confirms the follow-up. While a Stop hook is held, Codex shows "Working", and a person who types waits until they press Esc: the hook's call ends, its park closes (`close_conn_sinks`), and their UserPromptSubmit counts the follow-up as not run.
  - **A Stop wait that its own side ended (#449).** Esc, or the timeout above, ends the hook's call and closes its park (`close_conn_sinks`), and nothing can wake the session until its next hook. Before, Members showed an idle session as if it listened, until a message came and it read "parked" with the generic reason (`IDLE_WHY`). Now `close_conn_sinks` asks the session's adapter (`stop_wait_lost`, given how long the wait lasted) and, when it answers, the engine keeps that reason (`Engine.lost_stops`, by participant, memory only like the connection) and `parked_reason` gives it for as long as the session is idle with no wait open, a message waiting or not: Members shows it parked with "not listening: its Stop hook ended after 5 min (Esc, or Codex's tool_timeout_sec: docs/USAGE.md)". Any hook of the session's own ends it (the person typed, or Codex ran another turn; a subagent's, which the engine ignores, does not), and so does a Stop that waits again. Only Codex over HTTPS answers: a Claude Code listener comes back by itself after a dropped round, and a Cursor session is followed up at its stop hook. A session that is not idle when the close is seen is not marked. The broker sees the end of the call when it is cancelled or its connection closes. **Tests:** `tests/unit/test_https_stop_wait.py` (the reason and its length; gone at the next hook or a new wait, not at a subagent's hook; busy sessions, other harnesses and a session that left are never marked) and `tests/integration/test_agents_http_wake.py` (a Stop wait cancelled the way Codex's MCP client cancels one).
- **Claude Code** (`claude@https`, `HttpsClaudeAdapter`) is woken by its listener (#380's decision 3), a `command` hook with `asyncRewake` at SessionStart and Stop that runs the plugin's `bin/listen` (§49.5): POSIX `sh` and `curl`, waiting in rounds (on Windows the PowerShell script `bin/windows/listen.ps1`, which does the same: its rounds, statuses, markers and log are the shell script's). SessionStart runs `bin/session` (the report) and the listener side by side, since Claude Code starts a group's hooks together, and the listener starts even before the first turn on `startup` or `resume`. Either may reach the broker first: the report must not end the listener's park (#484; before, a resume lost that race most of the time and the member stayed deaf until its next Stop). A `/clear` or compaction may be mid-turn, so its SessionStart listener exits without posting; the next Stop starts one. A later Stop listener takes over the earlier round.
  - **A round.** It sends nothing but the session id (taken from the hook's input on the machine) and waits at `/mcp/listen` (`Listener`), which is in `AGENT_PATHS` behind the same door as `/mcp`. The broker treats the request as that session's Stop with `max_wait_s` from `X-Switchboard-Wait` (900 s), so it parks. A message answers 200 with the batch's text: the script prints it, exits 2, and Claude Code starts a turn with it, 32 ms later in the spike, idle until then with no spinner. The turn the wake starts confirms it with its first hook.
  - **The next round.** A round that runs out with nothing (or ends because its room was paused) answers 408, and the listener waits again at once, so an idle session stays reachable however long it stays idle. Its later rounds say so (`X-Switchboard-Again: 1`): that is the same listener coming back after a proxy cut its wait (a gateway's route timeout, a load balancer's idle timeout), and a refusal ends it for good, so the broker refuses one only when the session has really moved on (`MOVED_ON`): a turn started (busy, or waiting on an approval, which a message must never be pushed into) or the session ended (offline, or in no room). Any other state is treated as still listening, and the round parks (#436; before, any state but "idle" was refused, and a refusal left the session deaf with no trace). A round that slips in after the person started typing is still refused, and never reports the session idle. Each refusal is answered 409 `not_idle` with the state and the reason (or 410, for a session someone else ended) and logged at warning level (`listener refused for session …`); a first round, whose ordinary ends are a takeover or a hook, logs nothing. A message that comes between two rounds is pending, and the next round takes it as it opens.
  - **Told when someone else ended it.** A session in no room because something other than itself ended it (a kick, a closed room, a removed person or machine: anything but its own `leave` or quit) gets 410 with one line saying where and why (`AgentService.http_ended`). The listener prints it and exits 2, so the session learns it can't be reached instead of going deaf without a word (#402). It keeps the line in `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` and stays quiet when the same one comes back at later Stops, so it can't keep the session awake.
  - **The end.** A 409 ends the listener with exit 0, which wakes nothing: `nothing` when a newer listener or another hook of the session took over (every turn's ordinary end, not logged), `not_idle` when the broker refused a reconnect because the session had moved on (logged). Any other answer (a 401 for a refused token, a 403 or a page from a proxy in the way, a status nobody expected) used to end it just as silently; now it writes one line to `listen.log` in `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` (or `~/.cache/switchboard`): the time, the session, the round, the status and what the server said, cut short, kept under 64 KiB (past that, its last hundred lines). It also wakes the session once with the status and switchboard's own error word (`(unauthorized)`), never the rest of the body, which may be a proxy's page: the same way, and with the same once-only marker (`stopped-<session>`), as a 410. The same reason at later Stops stays quiet, so it can't keep the session awake; a wake by a message clears the marker. A server that can't be reached, or a 429 or 5xx the listener retries (500, 502, 503, 504), is not an end: it is tried again, after 1 s and doubling to 60 s, and at once when a long wait was cut (a proxy's idle timeout). The listener watches Claude Code's own pid (`CLAUDE_PID`, which Claude Code sets for what it runs), because a background hook outlives a Claude Code that quits (seen with 2.1.300): it stops within 2 s of it. The hook's `timeout` is 7 days, the longest an idle session stays reachable. A `claude -p` run waits for its background hooks, so one whose session joined a room keeps listening after it answers, until a message comes or it leaves; one that never joined ends at once (409).
  - **The token** reaches `curl` on its stdin (`-K -`), never its command line, where another process could read it. The PowerShell script puts it in a request header it builds itself, and starts no process. A Claude Code that signed in through the browser has no token the script can read, and presents a **listener key** instead (§49.11), which opens these two routes for one session and nothing else.
- **A busy session** gets priority messages as hook context after its tool calls, any tool's (#422) (Claude Code also at UserPromptSubmit and, after `/clear` or a compaction, SessionStart: the harness table, §7.3), and everything else at its next `read()`, `wait()` or `say()`.
- **Approvals.** Nothing is pushed into a running turn: a park opens only at Stop, and context rides back only on a hook's own reply, never to a session waiting on an approval (§7.4). So nothing can land on an open approval prompt. Claude Code's Notification hook shows the prompt to the room (§49.9).
- **Tests.** `tests/integration/test_agents_http_wake.py`: an idle Claude Code woken by its listener and confirmed by its next turn; a newer listener or another hook ending the old one with a 4xx; a round that runs out answering 408, and a later round parking only an idle session; the listener's door; an idle Codex woken through its Stop hook with `decision: block`; a Stop without `wait_s` only reporting idle; the approval mode; context for a busy session; a tool result's token confirming a pull answer; the reply shapes; a session that never joined, which a tool call's report writes nothing for; a switchboard tool's report with no result dropped and an empty result kept (#422); a report with no result confirming and taking back nothing; a subagent's hooks (`agent_id` set) delivering and confirming nothing and leaving an idle session's listener waiting, its PreToolUse report still naming the call's session, and what counts as a subagent's hook (#446). The same file for the listener's door (#436): a reconnecting round parking in "idle" and "starting" and refused, with the state and a warning in the broker's log, once the session is busy, waiting on an approval, offline or in no room; a first round never logged as a refusal.

### 49.5 The plugin, and Connect an agent (#384)
- **Claude Code's plugin** lives in this repository, `plugins/claude/`, and the repository is its marketplace (`.claude-plugin/marketplace.json`), so one line installs it: `/plugin install switchboard --marketplace amahpour/switchboard`. It has two settings (`userConfig`): the server's address, and the agent token, which is `sensitive`, so Claude Code keeps it as a secret rather than in a settings file, and optional: left empty, Claude Code signs in through the browser (§49.11). Its MCP server (`.mcp.json`) is `${user_config.server_url}/mcp` with `X-Switchboard-Token: ${user_config.token}` (never `Authorization`, which turns Claude Code's sign-in off, §49.11) and a `headersHelper`, `bin/headers`, that makes the listener key. Its hooks (`hooks/hooks.json`) are §49.4's, calling the plugin's own server (`plugin:switchboard:switchboard`), and PreToolUse matches switchboard's own tools only (`mcp__plugin_switchboard_switchboard__.*`). Its Notification hook (matcher `permission_prompt`) reports an open approval prompt (§49.9). Each of its PreToolUse, PostToolUse, PostToolUseFailure and Notification hooks also sends `agent_id`, so the server can tell a subagent's tool call from the session's own (§49.4, #446). After a call, PostToolUse and PostToolUseFailure each have two groups (#422): switchboard's own tools (that matcher), which carry their result, and every other tool (`^(?!mcp__plugin_switchboard_switchboard__)`), which carry no input or result (§49.4), so a busy agent hears its messages after any tool call and no other tool's output leaves the machine. Its SessionStart and Stop hooks run `bin/listen`, the listener: POSIX `sh`, `grep`, `sed` and `curl` on Linux and macOS, and PowerShell on Windows (below). It reads the settings from `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_SERVER_URL` and `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_TOKEN` (or, set up by hand, `SWITCHBOARD_URL` and `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN`; with no token, the listener key in the plugin's data folder, §49.11) and sends only the session id, after checking it is one (letters, digits, `-` and `_`). The token reaches `curl` on stdin (`-K -`), never its command line, where another process could read it.
- **Claude Code's plugin on native Windows (#440).** Claude Code's sessions run on Windows itself as well as under WSL2 (where the shell scripts run as on Linux). The hooks need a shell script and `curl` for the listener and the session report, which Windows has neither of by default, so the plugin carries a PowerShell version of each (`bin/windows/listen.ps1`, `session.ps1`) and the one `hooks.json` starts the right one in all three places. What decided it, checked with Claude Code 2.1.304 (the one the Claude desktop app bundles) on Windows 11 with Git for Windows, PowerShell 7.4 and Windows PowerShell 5.1 installed, and against Claude Code's hook documentation:
  - **Which shell runs a hook command.** In shell form (no `args`), `sh -c` on Linux and macOS, Git Bash on Windows, and PowerShell on Windows when Git Bash isn't installed; `"shell": "powershell"` forces PowerShell, and picked PowerShell 7 here with Windows PowerShell present too, even with PowerShell 7 off `PATH`. Exec form (`command` and `args`, no shell) needs a real executable on Windows, so there is none that Linux, macOS and Windows share. And no pair of hooks, one per platform, is possible: a hook with `"shell": "powershell"` fails on a machine without PowerShell ("no PowerShell executable"), where the hook then reports an error at every start. So one command string has to mean the same in `sh`, Git Bash and PowerShell. `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` is filled in with forward slashes (`C:/Users/…`), and a path with spaces or an apostrophe works in double quotes in all three. The hook gets the event's JSON on stdin and `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT`, `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA`, `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_*` and `CLAUDE_PID` (a Windows pid there) in its environment.
  - **The command.** `cd "${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/bin"; ./listen; exit $LASTEXITCODE`, and `./session` for the session report. `;` because Windows PowerShell 5.1 has no `&&`. `./listen` because a quoted path can't be started in PowerShell without its `&`, which `sh` can't parse: relative to `bin`, `sh` and Git Bash find the shell script `listen`, and PowerShell, which resolves a typed command by the extensions of the files beside it, finds `listen.cmd` (an extensionless file is not one of those; a `listen.ps1` here would win over the `.cmd`, and PowerShell's default policy refuses every `.ps1`, so the PowerShell scripts are in `bin/windows`). `exit $LASTEXITCODE` because PowerShell reports `-Command` as failed with 1 when the last native command exited with anything but 0, which loses the exit 2 that wakes the session (seen live: 2 became 1); in `sh` the unset variable vanishes and `exit` returns the last status. The listener's working folder is `bin`, which nothing in it uses.
  - **Git Bash.** Its `sh` can't run the shell listener: MSYS's `kill -0` doesn't see a Windows pid, so `bin/listen` found `CLAUDE_PID` gone and ended at once, silently (seen live: exit 0 in 0.4 s), and the session was never woken. On Git Bash, MSYS and Cygwin (`uname -s`), the shell scripts therefore start `windows/<name>.ps1` themselves, in Windows PowerShell with the profile skipped and the execution policy lifted for the one run (`-ExecutionPolicy Bypass`, as Claude Code's documentation does for a PowerShell hook), passing on stdin and the exit code, and only when that script is beside them: a script set up by hand without it runs as before. Elsewhere `uname` says Linux or Darwin and nothing changes. Without Git, `listen.cmd` and `session.cmd` start the same two commands.
  - **The scripts.** The same job, line for line: the same events and checks (only `Stop`, and `SessionStart` for `startup` and `resume`; a session id of letters, digits, `-` and `_`), the same settings, rounds, statuses, backoff, `listen.log` and once-only markers, and the same wake text. They read the hook's input as bytes and write the server's as bytes, so text is never put through the machine's code page (a message with an accent or an emoji arrives whole), and they are plain ASCII, because Windows PowerShell 5.1 reads a file without a byte-order mark in the ANSI code page. HTTP is .NET's `HttpClient` (no `curl.exe` to find; the system proxy and certificate store apply); a redirect is not followed, as curl's isn't, so the token goes only where the settings say; TLS 1.2 is added only where the framework's default is older. `CLAUDE_PID` is watched every 2 s, so the listener ends within seconds of Claude Code (seen live: 1 to 2 s), and without it the listener doesn't start and logs why. Any error ends a script with exit 0, never with a code Claude Code would read as a wake. The session report waits 5 s, and 2 s for a SessionEnd: Claude Code gives a quitting session's hooks as little as 1.5 s, and Windows PowerShell takes about half a second to start, so a slow machine can miss the report and show the member idle after Claude Code has gone (the shell script's budget is 1 s). Seen live, a SessionEnd took the member offline in Claude Code's own teardown.
  - **The header helper (#488).** `bin/headers` is a third script of the same kind (§49.11): `bin/headers.cmd` and, under Git Bash, `bin/headers` itself print `{}` and nothing else, so on native Windows a connection has no listener key and a sign-in works for the tools and the tool hooks but not for the idle wake or the session report. There is no PowerShell twin yet (a Windows machine to run it on was not at hand), and the typed token still does both.
  - **Line endings.** A plugin is installed by a `git` checkout on the user's machine, and Git for Windows checks out CRLF unless told otherwise (`core.autocrlf=true` is the installer's default); a shell script with CRLF fails in Git Bash. `.gitattributes` keeps the plugins' `bin` scripts LF and the `.cmd` files CRLF.
  - **Checked live** (Claude Code 2.1.304 and Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100 and PowerShell 7.4 on Windows 11, against a throwaway broker, #440): the two scripts under Windows PowerShell and PowerShell 7 through the four command lines Claude Code uses (directly, from Git Bash, and from Windows PowerShell and from PowerShell 7 as the hook shell); a real `claude.exe` running the plugin's own `hooks.json` with its stand-in model API, in Git Bash and with every hook forced into PowerShell: SessionStart reported idle, the listener parked, an @mention woke it (its text was in the next request to the model), and quitting took the member offline; the owner going away, a second listener taking over, a round running out, a refused token (logged, woken once, quiet after), and a server that is not there. **Not checked:** a session with a real model (the stand-in answered "ok"), the plugin's MCP server and `userConfig` (the checks gave the hooks the settings in `SWITCHBOARD_URL` and `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN`), and Windows PowerShell 5.1 as the hook shell inside a real `claude.exe` (it chose PowerShell 7; the same command line was run under 5.1 directly). **Known limits:** a Group Policy that sets the execution policy overrides `-ExecutionPolicy Bypass` (the script is refused, and the hook reports it), and constrained language mode (AppLocker, Windows Defender Application Control) refuses the .NET calls the scripts use (they end with exit 0 and, for the listener, a line in `listen.log`); in both the session can't be woken while idle. Codex's plugin is `mcp_tool` hooks and one POSIX `sh` script, `bin/approval` (§49.9), which has no PowerShell version: where there is no `sh` and `curl` it can't run, and the status of an open prompt isn't shown; nothing else of the plugin is affected. Cursor's and Devin's hooks are Python scripts started by a shell wrapper, and have not been run on Windows.
- **Codex's plugin** lives in `plugins/codex/`, and its marketplace is `.agents/plugins/marketplace.json`, which Codex reads before Claude Code's file: `codex plugin marketplace add amahpour/switchboard`, then `codex plugin add switchboard@switchboard`. It carries the hooks and one script (`.codex-plugin/plugin.json` names `./hooks/hooks.json`; `bin/approval` is the command hook for PermissionRequest, §49.9). Codex substitutes nothing in a plugin's MCP server file, so the server's address can't come from the plugin. The server is instead the user's own `[mcp_servers.switchboard]` in `config.toml` (`url`, `bearer_token_env_var` and `tool_timeout_sec = 86400`), which a plugin's server of that name would lose to anyway, and the hooks name it by that plain key. Its hook file keeps to Codex's rules: only `description` and `hooks` at the top, no null in an input, no `mcp_tool` hook at SessionEnd, which Codex skips, and in a template only fields its event always carries, since a missing one fails the hook. A normal Codex quit uses the MCP session DELETE instead (§49.2); SIGKILL leaves its last reported status. Each hook writes out its own event's name. The PermissionRequest hook is the one command hook: `"$PLUGIN_ROOT"/bin/approval` (POSIX `sh` and `curl`, as Claude Code's `bin/session`), `async`, 5 s at most. It reads the server's address from `SWITCHBOARD_URL` and the token from `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN` in Codex's environment, since a command can't read a plugin's or `config.toml`'s settings; the Connect card and USAGE tell the person to export both, and without them the hook does nothing (§49.9). PostToolUse has two groups (#422): `mcp__switchboard__.*`, with the result, and one with the matcher `""`, every tool, with none (Codex fires PostToolUse for its shell tool, named `Bash`, as well as for `apply_patch` and MCP tools, 0.158.0 live); the server drops the second report of a switchboard tool (§49.4). Codex runs a plugin's hooks only once the person trusts them; it asks at the next start, and again after a hook changes. The real Codex 0.158.0 took the marketplace from a local path and installed the plugin ("installed, enabled") in a throwaway `CODEX_HOME`.
- **A change to a plugin bumps its `version`**: that's how Claude Code and Codex know to update an installed copy, and a change that keeps the cached version never reaches anyone. A test enforces it (#402): it compares each plugin's files with the merge base with `origin/main` (in CI, with the first parent of the pull request's merge commit) and fails if a file under `plugins/claude/` or `plugins/codex/` changed or is new while its manifest's `version` is what it was. It skips where there is no history to compare with, as in the Docker test image.
- **Plugins update with a release, not with a merge** (#462). A marketplace is a file on `main`, and an installed copy follows `main` for it, so a plugin entry that was a path in the checkout (`./plugins/claude`) handed every plugin change to anyone who updated, or had auto-update on, the moment it merged: before the release and the server upgrade it needs. Claude Code's plugin 0.7.0 sent a field that the released server refuses, and `claude plugin update` would have installed it hours before the server it needed. Each marketplace now lists its plugin as a `git-subdir` source of this repository (`https://github.com/amahpour/switchboard.git`, the plugin's folder) at a `ref`, the tag of the latest release. The marketplace file keeps following `main`; the plugin's files come from the tag, and the `version` in the `plugin.json` there decides whether a copy is new. A `github` or `url` source would take the repository's root, and a `release` branch for people to add the marketplace from (the other design) would leave every install made from `main` following `main`. Checked live with Claude Code 2.1.305 and Codex 0.158.0, each against a throwaway config directory and a stand-in repository:
  - **A merge changes nothing.** With a plugin change on `main` and the marketplace unchanged, Claude Code's `marketplace update` and `plugin update` say the plugin is "already at the latest version", and Codex's `marketplace upgrade` leaves the installed version alone.
  - **A release changes it.** `release.py` moves the `ref` of both files to the new tag in the release PR (`pin_marketplace`), so the tag and the marketplace move together, and stops the release if a file has no plugin pinned to a tag. The two files are release files (`is_release_file`), so the release PR still skips the suite. After the tag exists, Claude Code's update installs the release's plugin, and Codex's session start does: it upgrades a configured Git marketplace and refreshes the installed plugins from it, as part of its plugin start-up, with no switch per marketplace.
  - **Claude Code's auto-update is the person's to turn on** (`/plugin` → Marketplaces → switchboard → Enable auto-update; off by default for a marketplace the person added), and runs after the first message of a session, with a delay of up to ten minutes. Its docs: [marketplace sources](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/plugins/marketplace-reference#plugin-sources) (`ref`, `sha`), [adding a marketplace at a ref](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/plugins/install#add-a-marketplace) (`owner/repo#ref`), [keeping plugins updated](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/plugins/install#keep-plugins-updated). Codex: [`source` types and `codex plugin marketplace add owner/repo --ref`](https://developers.openai.com/plugins/build/plugins).
  - **The minute before the tag.** `release.yml` makes the tag after the merge, so for about a minute the marketplace names a tag that is not there. An update in that minute fails and keeps the plugin that is installed (Claude Code: "Remote branch … not found", the plugin stays enabled; Codex: "failed to materialize plugin source", the same). Claude Code installs the release's plugin at its next update. Codex has already marked the marketplace current, so it does so when it next lists its plugins (the `/plugins` browser), or at the first session start after another change merges to `main`. A Codex session that started inside that minute can therefore run the old plugin for a while; the old plugin works with the new server, so this is bounded and harmless, and not worth a second tag or a change to the workflow.
  - **An install made before this adopts it** with its next marketplace update (tried from the path form in both harnesses), with nothing for the person to redo.
  - **The server goes first.** The pin keeps a plugin from arriving before its release; it cannot know which server a machine talks to, so USAGE and the release notes say to upgrade the server before the plugin. A plugin that left out fields an older server refuses would need to know the server's version, and an `mcp_tool` hook's input is a fixed template, so it is not built.
  - **A contributor trying an unreleased plugin** loads the folder (`claude --plugin-dir plugins/claude`) or, for Codex, a throwaway marketplace file with a `local` source, as these files had before #462.
- **What the join says** (#402): a join over HTTPS tells the agent which switchboard version it is talking to, that its Stop hook waits on switchboard and wakes it when idle, that while it works priority messages come as context after its tool calls, and that the rest wait for its next `read()`, `wait()` or `say()`. It no longer says switchboard can't wake it, which is the text for a harness with no push path.
- **Kept in step by tests** (`tests/integration/test_agent_plugins.py`): a changed plugin has a new version (above); each marketplace lists its plugin as a `git-subdir` of this repository at the tag of the release the checkout is at, in the plugin's folder and under its manifest's name, and where the checkout has that tag, the plugin at it has that name (#462); Codex's plugin has no server, and its hooks keep to Codex's rules; the token is sensitive and optional, goes in `X-Switchboard-Token` and never in `Authorization`, the server is its address's `/mcp`, and the helper's command is one string all three shells understand (§49.11); every hook calls the plugin server's `hook` tool with only fields it takes (`HOOK_FIELDS`, which the tool's own parameters are built from), PreToolUse matches switchboard's tools only, every tool matches exactly one PostToolUse group in Claude Code's plugin, the group for other tools sends nothing but the event, the session, the call and the tool's name with a timeout of 2 s at most, and filled from each harness's recorded payload with a secret in everything the tool did, no secret reaches it (#422), the recorded requests of Claude Code 2.1.305 (for the session's own call and for a subagent's) and Codex 0.158.0 for that group (`tests/fixtures/mcp_http/*_hook_post_tool*.json`) are what the templates send, and a busy member hears an @mention in the answer to them, every tool hook of Claude Code's plugin passes `agent_id` (an empty string for the session's own calls, the id for a subagent's, filled from the recorded payloads, and never `agent_type`, which a `claude --agent` session's own calls carry), a subagent's tool call takes nothing from the session while its own call does (#446), and SessionStart and Stop run the listener in the background; the server takes every field at once; and `bin/listen` itself (and its PowerShell twin, under `pwsh` where it is installed: `tests/integration/test_agent_plugins.py` runs each of the listener's and the session script's tests against both), run as Claude Code runs it against a broker, exits 2 with the message and 0 with nothing else (superseded, a session that never joined, a session id that isn't one, a wrong token when it has no folder to say so in), with the token never in `curl`'s arguments; against a stand-in server it logs a refusal to `listen.log` and wakes the session once with its status and error word and never the body (#436), says nothing for a takeover and logs but doesn't wake for a busy refusal, and keeps its log small; run behind a gateway that cuts its rounds mid-wait, as Envoy's route timeout does, it posts again and is still woken by a message; it waits again when a round runs out, rides out a server that is away, says which round it is, and ends with Claude Code (`tests/unit/test_deploy_ingress.py` keeps the Kubernetes Ingress's timeouts longer than a round). A stand-in Claude resumes and hears an @mention before any prompt or Stop, with the SessionStart report arriving before the listener's round or after it (`tests/unit/test_engine_session_start_park.py` holds the engine's side), while `/clear` and compaction SessionStart events make no listen request (#412, #484). `claude plugin validate` passes on the plugin and on the marketplace. `tests/integration/test_claude_plugin_windows.py` keeps Windows working without a Windows machine: every command hook is one of the two command strings, which in `sh` (with a plugin folder with spaces and an apostrophe in its name, from another working folder) start the script with the hook's input and return its code 0, 1 and 2; Git Bash's hand-over, with stand-ins for `uname` and `powershell.exe` (it starts the PowerShell script with the arguments above, and the token in none; Linux and macOS and a script without its PowerShell twin don't); `bin` holds nothing PowerShell would find before `listen.cmd`; the `.cmd` files; the PowerShell scripts are ASCII with no PowerShell 7-only operator, start no process and name the token only to read it and put it in the header; a redirect is not followed; a wake message with accents and an emoji arrives whole; the session script's request, header and printed context; and the line endings `.gitattributes` gives each script.
- **Connect an agent.** The card that shows a new token once, in Settings → Agents, also shows how to connect an agent on that device, each line with Copy. For Claude Code: the line to run in it, and this switchboard's own address (the page's origin). For Codex: the two plugin commands, the `config.toml` entry with this switchboard's `/mcp`, and `export SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN=` with the token. It goes when the token does.

### 49.6 Cursor over HTTPS (#394)

Cursor Agent CLI 3.25.6 initializes `/mcp` as `Cursor` 1.0.0. Its test account reached its usage limit before a tool call, so the spike records `sessionStart` and `sessionEnd` but cannot claim a native call ID. The plugin instead gives its HTTP tools distinct `switchboard_` names. Cursor's documented `preToolUse.updated_input` puts the hook's `conversation_id` and `tool_use_id` into that tool call's arguments. Before returning the rewrite, the command hook reports those two IDs and the tool name to `/mcp/pretool` under the same agent token. The broker accepts the call only when all three fields match one unexpired, one-use report for that token. It strips the two private arguments before the ordinary tool schema runs. A missing hook, a failed report, a reused ID and a crossed conversation fail closed. The tool aliases keep this rewrite away from other MCP tools. No ordering or active-conversation guess is used.

Cursor's command hooks report session start/end, tool results and Stop to `/mcp/event`. A tool's result goes as the batch tokens found in its output and nothing else: the broker reads only those, and a `wait()` answer with a few messages is over the 4 KB a hook request may be, which the broker refuses (413), so the answer was never confirmed and went out again (§8.7). The `cursor@https` adapter keys each member by `cursor@<device>:<conversation id>` from the checked call, and its Stop hook parks the session at Stop (§9.4). Hook replies use Cursor's `additional_context` and `followup_message` shapes. The command hook sends the token only in an HTTP header; a stopped hook may wait 630 seconds. The plugin requires Python 3 and a URL and token in its process environment for hooks, in addition to its MCP variables. The broker's agent-token door covers both new hook paths.

### 49.7 Devin over HTTPS (#394)

Devin CLI 3000.11.92 reads project `.devin/config.json` and `.devin/mcp_config.json`. Its HTTP MCP transport says `rmcp` 3.2.0 in every call's `_meta` and sends a progress token, but neither the session ID nor the hook's tool-use ID. The project PreToolUse hook's `hookSpecificOutput.updatedInput` was observed adding both IDs to the actual MCP call, in two simultaneous Devin conversations on one device. The `switchboard_https` hook command now reports that call to `/mcp/pretool` before printing the rewrite. As for Cursor (§49.6), the broker requires an exact one-use match under the same agent token, strips both private arguments before tool validation, and refuses a call with a missing or crossed report. The generic `rmcp` client name alone never names a session. The `devin@https` adapter uses the checked session ID in `devin@<device>:<session id>` and otherwise keeps Devin's wait loop, Stop re-arm and context behavior. Command hooks report status through `/mcp/event`; the broker returns only the existing Devin context and Stop shapes. The template's PermissionRequest hook (`bin/hook permission`) tells the broker that a prompt is open and prints nothing at all (§49.9). The hook needs Python 3 and gets the address and token from its process environment, without logging or putting the token in its command line.

Three things the first live run with Devin CLI 3000.11.92 showed, each now fixed and tested. (1) The wait loop's checks (§9.5) compare the tool's name, and `devin@https` calls it `mcp__switchboard_https__wait`, not `mcp__switchboard__wait`; both hook reports name it so (the PreToolUse report the broker passes on gets the prefix, PostToolUse already has it), or a wait() answer is never confirmed, is taken back as an orphan's, and comes again. (2) The PostToolUse hook sends only the batch tokens found in a switchboard tool's answer. The broker reads nothing else of it, and a long wait() answer would not fit the 4 KB a hook request may be. (3) Devin's HTTP client sends a call again after 120 s without an answer. The first send spent the one-use report, so the repeat is refused ("no matching session hook") and the first wait() is left open. A wait() over HTTPS is therefore capped at 100 s (`DEVIN_WAIT_CAP_S`), in what the server clamps, in the join text and in the Stop re-arm. An idle Devin listens in rounds of 100 s, and its re-arms are the ones §9.5 allows per prompt and per hour, not the 600 s the config allows.

**A Devin that was killed (#448).** A Devin ended by SIGHUP (closing its terminal or `tmux kill-session`) or SIGTERM exits within a second and runs **no SessionEnd hook** (Devin CLI 3000.11.92, live; a normal `/exit` does, within a second: `tests/fixtures/payloads/devin/https_exit_signals.json`). Its Members row then stayed `busy` for good, and messages to it waited for a turn that would never end. What the broker does see is its open `wait()` losing its connection: the MCP tool call is cancelled, `AgentService.unwait` closes the wait as it always did, and now also calls `Engine.wait_dropped`. For an adapter that asks (`wait_drop_means_gone`: `devin@https` only) that starts a clock; any hook of the session, or a new `wait()`, ends it. A live Devin re-arms at once, since every call of the plugin's server is bracketed by hooks. The tick (`rules.wait_drop_expired`, after `WAIT_DROP_GRACE_S`, 130 s: one wait round and a margin) takes a session that stayed silent **offline**, with status source `wait:dropped`. Its membership stays, as for a Codex thread that quit (§49.2). Its next hook of any kind brings it back to `busy` (`gone_by_drop`), so a resumed Devin, or one whose connection only broke for a while, is back before its first `wait()` is answered; a wait that ends in a timeout, which the server answers, never counts as a drop. The state is memory only: a broker restart forgets a pending clock. Not covered: a Devin whose connection never closes (a machine that lost power or its network) leaves no drop to see, and stays as it was until something is heard from it. And a wait cancelled by a live Devin that then sits silent at its prompt for 130 s shows offline until its next hook; Devin sends no cancel for an interrupt (F§6 5.6), so this was not seen. **Tests:** `tests/unit/test_devin_adapter.py` (a dropped wait with no call after it goes offline at 130 s and not before, its room kept; a hook or a new wait ends the clock; 40 listening rounds of 100 s that end in timeouts never count; any hook brings an offline-for-a-drop session back and delivers what waited; no other harness starts the clock; a SessionEnd leaves nothing behind) and `tests/integration/test_agents_http_devin.py` (two Devins on one token: one whose wait connection really drops goes offline as the clock passes, one that keeps re-arming never does, and the first is busy again with its next hook).

### 49.8 What retired, and where it went (#385)

Phase 4 of #380 removes the older ways an agent reached a broker, one pull request at a time; the last release that has each is v0.37.2 (`git show v0.37.2:docs/DESIGN.md`). The first took out the links:

- **SSH remotes and the satellite (§27), machines that dial in and pairing (§31.7, §31.8).** The code, the `/link` routes, `/api/remotes`, `/api/machines` and the web UI's Remotes and Machines are gone. An agent on another machine is an agent over HTTPS with its device's token (§49.1, §49.3).
- **Four verbs keep their names.** `switchboard remote`, `satellite`, `join` and (since the second pull request, §9.7) `install` print one line to stderr, saying what was retired in 0.38.0 and where agents connect now, and exit 2, whatever arguments they are given (`cli.RETIRED`; `main` uses `parse_known_args` for them alone, so every other verb still refuses an argument it doesn't know). An `authorized_keys` line that runs `switchboard satellite`, a script and an old harness entry learn what happened, not "invalid choice". Test: `tests/unit/test_cli_retired.py`.
- **Schema 29** drops the tables `remotes` and `link_machines` and ends the sessions that only those links could reach: a participant with a host and a real MCP pid (an HTTPS session has a device host and pid 0, so it is never touched). Each ends as a session end does (`ended_at`, status `offline`, `status_src = 'retired'`; its memberships leave with reason `retired`, its claims and open deliveries go). The step runs in the migration's one transaction after the verified backup (`switchboard.db.v28.bak`, §4), and the row-count comparison after it covers the tables that stay. Test: `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v29.py`, from a database a 0.37.2 broker wrote (`tests/fixtures/db/v0_37_2.sql`, made by `make_v0_37_2.py`): every row that should stay is there, only the two retired hosts' sessions end, and a failed step leaves the database at 28.
- **Files in a home** (`remotes.toml`, `remotes/`, `link/`, `satellite.toml`) are not read and not deleted; the release notes list them.
- **`switchboard install` (§9.7) and `update`'s re-registering (§45).** An agent over HTTPS registers nothing with its harness's own config but the plugin (§49.5), so `install <harness|all>`, `--print-args` and `--allow-editable` are retired, and `install` prints the same one line and exits 2. `uninstall` stays for what an older install wrote; its tests start from static fixtures of that (`tests/fixtures/install/`, made by the 0.37.2 installer). The live tests that drove real CLIs with `install --print-args` are deleted; their successors drive the CLIs over HTTPS (§12.4, #470).

The third took out the local agent path, which is everything that reached the broker another way than HTTPS:

- **The per-session MCP server and the hook script.** `switchboard mcp` (a stdio server per agent session, with its `mcp.hello` claims of version and tools, `mcp.attach` and the credential it kept in memory) and `switchboard hook` (the debug verb for the script) print the same one line as the others and exit 2. `src/switchboard/hook/` is gone, with the content-addressed copies the broker wrote, the 60-second re-hash and the `hook_hash` event; the table of what a hook may be answered with moved to `broker/hook_replies.py` (§7.3) with its invariant test.
- **The socket methods and roles** `mcp.*`, `agent.*`, `hook.*`, and the `mcp`, `member` and `hook` roles (§5.2). `peer.py` keeps the human CLI check; `verify_mcp_peer`, `resolve_hook_participant` and `proc.argv` for one process went.
- **Claude's inbox and its session registry,** the Codex app-server link (`codex_rpc`, the thread proof, the liveness guard, the daemon-restart re-bind and `codex queue`), and the Cursor and Devin local adapters. Every adapter is for a session over HTTPS now (§9); there is no push route, no `send()`, no `Push` action and no `hosts.py` (what the local machine's `/proc` could say about a pid). The Devin logic is the one adapter `devin@https` (§9.5).
- **Schema 30** (§4) ends every session that reached the broker another way than HTTPS (`left_reason='retired'`, as for 29), drops `participants.agent_pid`, `agent_start`, `mcp_pid`, `mcp_start`, `claude_socket`, `bind_state`, `bind_nonce`, `thread_proof`, `env_leak`, `last_loop_count`, `unconfirmed_followups`, `push_expiries`, the index on the MCP pid, and `memberships.cred_hash`. Restart recovery no longer probes a pid. The step runs in the migration's one transaction after the verified backup, which one 28 to 30 upgrade writes once (`switchboard.db.v28.bak`; from 29 it is `.v29.bak`). Test: `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v30.py`, from the same 0.37.2 database as 29's.
- **Config keys** of the local path (§2) are accepted and ignored, with one warning at start.
- **Things this left to say.** `/catchup` finds a session's id through the adapter of its device, so Claude Code, Codex and Cursor sessions over HTTPS have one (§26); before, only the local adapters answered, and an HTTPS Claude session read "unknown harness". `report` labels a Stop hook's follow-up by the tier its session joined with, not `cursor:stop-park` (§12.6). The `waiting-approval` status had no source after this (§7.4); #472 gave it one (§49.9).
- **Tests.** The scripted test agent is an MCP client over HTTPS (§9.6); the rules and engine tests build sessions over HTTPS (`tests/engine_agents.py`), and `tests/engine_sim.py` simulates a held Stop hook and a `wait()` loop (§12.2). The tests of the removed code (the hook script, the inbox, the app-server, the stand-in harness processes) are deleted, and the live tests of agents over HTTPS are §12.4.

### 49.9 An open approval prompt, shown to the room (#472)

Two sources set `waiting-approval` before 0.38.0 (Claude's session registry, the Codex app-server's thread status). Both went with the local path, and nothing set the status over HTTPS: a session stuck at its person's prompt looked `busy`. **What it is for** (the maintainer, on the issue, 2026-10-21): "the point is that other agents can see it", so that they "can choose to move on or wait. I like to give the agents autonomy to make that decision." It is information for the room, not a delivery guard over HTTPS, where a message reaches a session only as the answer to its own call or hook and so can never land in a prompt (§11, item 7), and a room message cannot answer one.

- **Which hook.** Checked live with Claude Code 2.1.305 and 2.1.307 (haiku, default mode, a throwaway broker, the plugin loaded from the checkout, a logger on every event): when a Bash call needs permission, PreToolUse fires, then **PermissionRequest**, then, within 12 ms, **Notification** with `notification_type: "permission_prompt"` (`tests/fixtures/payloads/claude/Notification_permission_prompt.json`; the old `PermissionRequest.json` is the other). Claude Code's plugin uses the Notification: it reports a prompt and can decide nothing, while PermissionRequest can approve, and switchboard registers none for Claude Code (§7.1, §11). Claude Code sends other Notification types too (`idle_prompt`, `auth_success`, `elicitation_dialog` and more): the plugin's matcher is `permission_prompt`, and the broker checks the type again (`Adapter.asks_approval`, `AgentService.http_hook`), so no other type does anything, to the status or to the hold. Its hook template sends the event, the session, `agent_id` and the type; never the message, the tool or its input. `notification_type` is a new field of the hook tool (`HOOK_FIELDS`), so the server goes first (§49.5). The reply is always `{}` (§7.3).
- **What clears it.** The next hook of the session's own thread, by its own rule (§7.4). Live: approve, and the tool's PostToolUse comes at once (then Stop; 2.1.305 and 2.1.307). **Decline** (Esc, or "No"; 2.1.305) and **no hook comes** until the person's next prompt (UserPromptSubmit): not PostToolUseFailure, not Stop, not PermissionDenied. The status therefore stays for as long as the session is waiting on its person, which after a decline is still true (Claude Code says "What should Claude do instead?"). It is not a timeout. The PreToolUse of the same call comes before the prompt, so it cannot clear it, and the plugin sends PreToolUse for switchboard's own tools only (§49.3).
- **Subagents.** Claude Code runs the session's hooks for a subagent's tool calls (§49.4, #446), and sends the Notification for a subagent's prompt **with no `agent_id`** (live: the PermissionRequest of the same prompt names the subagent). So a prompt in a subagent shows the session as waiting, which is true of its person. A subagent's own hooks still settle nothing for the session, with one exception: a tool that finishes in a subagent (PostToolUse or PostToolUseFailure with an `agent_id`) ends the hold (`Engine.subagent_tool_ran`), because otherwise a subagent that runs for minutes after its prompt was approved would keep the session "waiting" the whole time. It changes nothing else: no confirmation, no context, no listener ended. A subagent's tool finishing while another prompt is still open ends the hold early (the status falls back to `busy`, what it was before #472).
- **A listener.** A Notification is a hook of the session, so it ends an idle session's open listener like any other (§49.4); a background subagent's prompt in an idle session is the case, and the listener starts again at the session's next Stop.
- **Who sees it.** `who` adds what it means to the member's line: "paused at its person's approval prompt; it answers nobody until they act. Wait for it or move on: your call" (`WAITING_APPROVAL_WHO`). The join text of every harness over HTTPS adds the same, shorter (`APPROVAL_GUIDANCE`, about 150 characters; the test agent's join, which the 6,000-character budget test measures, is unchanged). Neither says which to choose. Members and the Inspector show "Waiting for approval", and a review board's card for the agent whose move it is shows the same in words and amber, where it showed "working" (§37). `switchboard report` counts a prompt open for more than 60 seconds as a stall (§12.6).
- **The other harnesses: Codex and Devin report it too, with a hook that cannot decide.** Codex 0.158.0 and Devin CLI 3000.11.92 fire PreToolUse and then **PermissionRequest** at an approval prompt and nothing else (Codex also an Interrupt when the prompt is declined with Esc; Devin nothing); neither has a Notification event, and Devin's did not fire. Their only hook is therefore a permission hook, which was left unregistered until the maintainer's yes of 2026-10-21 (§11 item 2): asked whether to add a report-only PermissionRequest hook for them that "can never approve or deny anything", the answer was "Do this". It reuses this section's path: `asks_approval` in both adapters (`ev.ev == "PermissionRequest"`), the same status, the same clearing, the same `who` and join text. Cursor Agent 2026.10.18 has no event at an approval prompt at all (21 events; its import of Claude hooks maps PermissionRequest and Notification to nothing; `beforeShellExecution` and `beforeMCPExecution` run before every command, prompt or not), so it still looks `busy`, and docs/USAGE.md says so.
- **Why a command that discards the reply (checked live, Codex 0.158.0 in a throwaway `CODEX_HOME` and Devin 3000.11.92 with a project-level config in a temp directory).** A PermissionRequest hook's output is the permission decision in both harnesses. Codex's docs say an `mcp_tool` hook has "the same output contract as a command hook", and it does: with a stand-in server that answered the hook tool with `{"hookSpecificOutput":{"hookEventName":"PermissionRequest","decision":{"behavior":"allow"}}}`, Codex ran the command with no prompt. So a plugin hook of the kind every other switchboard hook is (`mcp_tool`, whose output is the server's reply) could be made to approve by a hostile server, and the report-only rule would hold only while the server was honest. The same decision printed by an `async` command hook (after 2 s) did nothing: the prompt stayed and the command did not run, as Codex documents ("can't block, approve, rewrite, or otherwise control"). Devin CLI 3000.11.92 does the same with a command hook: one that printed `{"decision":"approve"}` had the command run with no prompt (its docs define a top-level `decision` and don't say what a PermissionRequest hook's output does). And the shipped hooks, run by the real Codex and the real Devin against a server that answered every report with an allow and an approve, left the prompt as it was. So both plugins' hooks are commands that print nothing, and `/mcp/event` is their only door: the `hook` tool answers `{}` and does nothing for a PermissionRequest (an `mcp_tool` hook of this event is refused, not served).
- **What the hooks are.** *Codex:* `plugins/codex/bin/approval`, a command hook in `hooks/hooks.json` (`"command": "\"$PLUGIN_ROOT\"/bin/approval"`, `async`, `timeout` 5), POSIX `sh` and `curl`. It takes the thread's id from the payload with `grep` (and sends it only if it is letters, digits, `-` and `_`), posts `{"harness":"codex","event":"PermissionRequest","session_id":…}` to `$SWITCHBOARD_URL/mcp/event` with the token on curl's stdin, sends curl's output to `/dev/null`, prints nothing and exits 0. The plugin is 0.4.0; Codex asks the person to review its hooks again. *Devin:* `bin/hook permission` (`plugins/devin/lib/hook.py`, `report_permission`): the same request with `"harness":"devin"`, 2 s at most, every error swallowed, nothing printed. The harness gives the hook the tool and its input (`tool_input.command` and the rest); neither script forwards them. Both need `SWITCHBOARD_URL` (the server's address without `/mcp`) and `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN` in the harness's environment: Devin's other hooks already did, and for Codex it is one more line in the Connect card (Settings → Agents) and USAGE. A Codex set up before this, with the plugin updated and no `SWITCHBOARD_URL`, works as it did and isn't shown as waiting.
- **What the server does with a report.** `CommandHook` (`/mcp/event`) takes `harness: codex` for this one event (any other event, and `/mcp/pretool`, are refused with 400), reads the session id of the body and no other field, and answers `200 {}` in every state, whatever happened to the session. `AgentService.http_hook` drops any output the engine hands a report-only event (`models.REPORT_ONLY_EVENTS`) and acknowledges nothing; the engine hands none, in any state of the session (`tests/unit/test_engine_core.py`). A report from a session that never joined is inert.
- **What clears it, per harness (live).** *Codex:* approve, and the command runs and the tool's PostToolUse comes (status → `busy`), then Stop (→ `idle`); decline with Esc ("You canceled the request"), and no hook comes, so the status stays until the person's next prompt (UserPromptSubmit → `busy`). Codex runs a subagent's tool hooks with the thread's session id and the subagent's own turn id (§49.4, #446), so a prompt in a subagent shows the thread as waiting, and the subagent's finished tool is the only sign that it was answered: `Engine._claim_for_hook` hands a hook that `other_thread` recognises to `Engine.subagent_tool_ran` (it ends the hold and does nothing else), as the HTTP layer does for Claude Code's subagents (and, as there, a subagent's tool finishing while the thread's own prompt is still open ends the hold early). This case is tested in the engine and not live: asked to run a command through a subagent, Codex's model declined to. *Devin:* approve, and the command runs with no hook of Devin's own, because its hooks run for switchboard's own tools only (§49.7): the status ends at Devin's next hook, which is its next call of a switchboard tool or its Stop (in the live run, at the end of a one-command turn). A long approved command followed by many non-switchboard tools therefore keeps the status for as long as the turn runs. For both, a report that reaches the server after the prompt was already answered (a slow network and a quick person; Codex's hook is `async`, so it never holds the prompt) shows until the session's next hook. Decline with Esc ("Canceled due to user interrupt"), and no hook comes, as for Codex.
- **Tests.** `tests/unit/test_engine_core.py` (the hook sets the status and only for the right type; each next hook clears it by its own rule; the message that waited arrives in the clearing hook; a subagent's tool ends the hold and nothing else; for Codex and Devin: PermissionRequest sets it, every next hook clears it, the engine answers a report with nothing in every state, the other harnesses take no PermissionRequest, a Codex subagent's finished tool ends the hold), `tests/unit/test_permission_hooks_static.py` (every plugin's hook file is read: only Codex's and Devin's register a permission hook, one each, as a command, `async` in Codex; both scripts run against a server that answers with every decision it can and print nothing, exit 0, send the session id and the event's name and no tool or input, and neither hangs on a silent server), `tests/integration/test_approval_report_only.py` (the server's half: the report sets the status and is answered `{}`, in every state with a message waiting; only the session id is read; Codex's door takes this event and no other; the `hook` tool refuses it; whatever the engine hands back is dropped), `tests/integration/test_agent_plugins.py` (the plugin's Notification group, its fields against the recorded request and payload; Claude Code's and Cursor's plugins register no PermissionRequest; end to end with the recorded request: Members, `who` and the join text, the held message, the clearing PostToolUse; other types ignored; a subagent's Notification ignored), `tests/e2e/test_web_ui.py` (Members, the Inspector and the board card, on a phone too) and `tests/e2e/test_agent_tokens_ui.py` (Codex's Connect steps give the address and the token).

### 49.10 Signing an agent in through the browser (#386)

Decision 1 of #380 made a token per device first and browser sign-in later. This is later, for the two harnesses whose MCP client does OAuth: Codex (`codex mcp login`) and Claude Code (`/mcp`, Authenticate). The person types and copies nothing; the agent starts the sign-in, and the person approves it in the browser. The rules are `broker/oauth.py`, the routes are in `web.py`, and the consent page is `web/static/oauth.html` and `oauth.js`. **What an agent ends up with is an agent token (§49.1)**: nothing about `/mcp` changes after it is made, and an agent that uses a token by hand is untouched, and so are the `curl` hooks of Cursor and Devin, which cannot use OAuth. Claude Code's listener and session report can't either, and use a key of their own (§49.11).

**What the clients do** (a spike against a logging stand-in server, 2026-10-21; then both against a real broker, below). Claude Code 2.1.305 and 2.1.307, after a 401 with `resource_metadata`, and Codex 0.158.0, after a bare `GET /mcp`, read `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource`, then `/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` at the root; register by dynamic client registration (`token_endpoint_auth_method: none`, `application_type: native`, `grant_types` `authorization_code` and `refresh_token`, and a loopback redirect: Claude Code `http://localhost:<port>/callback` and a client name of "Claude Code (<server name>)", Codex `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback/<random>` and "Codex"); send the browser to the authorization endpoint with PKCE `S256`, `state` and `resource=<the /mcp URL>`, and no `scope`; and swap the code with a form-encoded `POST` of `client_id`, `code_verifier`, `redirect_uri` and `resource`. Neither used client ID metadata documents, and **both took a token answer with no `expires_in` and no refresh token** and called the server with it. Claude Code skips OAuth for any server that has a static `Authorization` header (see "Claude Code and the plugin" below).

**The flow**
1. An agent with no live token calls `/mcp` and is told, in the 401's `WWW-Authenticate`, where its metadata is (§49.2).
2. `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (and the same at `/mcp`, RFC 9728) names this server's `/mcp` as the resource and itself as its one authorization server. `GET /.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` (RFC 8414) says what it does: `S256` only, `none` for client authentication, `authorization_code` only. Both are built from the web origin (§30), never from the request's Host.
3. `POST /oauth/register` (RFC 7591): `oauth.register` stores a name and the redirect URIs and answers a `client_id` (`sbc_` and 16 characters), with no secret. Other metadata is read for errors and then dropped.
4. The agent opens `GET /oauth/authorize?…` in the person's browser. The broker checks the request (`oauth.parse_authorize`). A client it does not know or a `redirect_uri` the client did not register gets a 400 page and no redirect; any other mistake (no PKCE or `plain`, another `response_type`, a `resource` that is not this `/mcp`, a bad `state`) is sent back to the agent's registered address as `error=…&state=…`, so it stops waiting. A good request gets the same static page for everyone, and, when the browser has no session, a sealed `switchboard_oauth_next` cookie (HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, Secure behind https, ten minutes) holding the query. Signing in lands on `/`, which sends the browser on to `/oauth/authorize` with that query, once; the cookie is only a way back, because the consent page and every route behind it check the request again.
5. `oauth.js` asks `GET /api/oauth/consent?<the same query>`, which checks the request again and answers `signed_in: false` (a 200: the page asks again every three seconds, so a sign-in in another tab, or `switchboard login` on a desktop, carries it on, and a refused request would be a console error each time), or who is signed in, what the agent calls itself, where it will answer, and whether a password or passkey check is still wanted.
6. **The consent card** says: who is signed in; what the agent calls itself (its own claim, shown as text); that its answer goes to "a program on this computer" at a loopback address; a **Name this device** field (the rule of Settings → Agents, checked in the page and again in the broker); what approving grants (act as your agents on that device; not sign in here, post as you or run a command; approve only if you just started this) and where to revoke it; **Approve** and **Deny**. On a hosted broker with no password or passkey check in the last five minutes, a Confirm it's you step (the password, or **Use a passkey**) comes first, as for Make a token (§31.6, §32.4).
7. **Approve** is `POST /api/oauth/approve {query, device}`. It needs a person's session (a cookie: an agent token is no session), the page's `Origin`, and `X-Switchboard: 1` (the same rule as every web write, §5.4), a fresh check on a hosted broker, a request that passes `parse_authorize` again, and a device `agent_tokens.free` accepts (a host name, not held by anyone's live token or sign-in, the person under their cap of 20). It makes a code and answers `{redirect}`: the registered address with `code` and `state`. The page opens it only if it is a loopback address (`http://localhost|127.0.0.1|[::1]:<port>/…`), whatever the broker said. **Deny** (`POST /api/oauth/deny`) answers `access_denied` and the state the same way and makes nothing.
8. `POST /oauth/token` (`oauth.exchange`): the code, its PKCE verifier, the client and the redirect it was made for, and a `resource` if one is sent. It makes the device's agent token (`agent_tokens.make_signin`: `via='oauth'`, the agent's name) and answers `{access_token, token_type: "Bearer"}`, `no-store`. It answers 400 with RFC 6749's `error` and `error_description` otherwise (`invalid_request`, `invalid_grant`, `invalid_target`, `unsupported_grant_type`).

**Codes** are 32 random bytes, kept in memory only as their sha256 (`OAuthState`), good for 60 seconds, once, at most 256 at a time. A code is **one try**: asking for it removes it, so a wrong verifier burns it. A code presented again after it was spent revokes the token it made (RFC 6749 §10.5) and ends that device's sessions, and says so in the `agent_tokens` events (`why`). A restart forgets every code, which costs a person one more click.

**Clients** (`oauth_clients`, schema 31) are only needed for the few minutes a sign-in takes, so `oauth_client_add` removes the ones older than a day and the oldest past 500 as it adds one. Registration takes no sign-in, so it is limited to 60 a minute for everyone (429 `temporarily_unavailable`); someone who can reach the server can therefore keep new agents from registering for a minute at a time, the same class as flooding sign-ins (§32.6). A body is at most 8 KB, read with a cap before it is parsed. Only **public clients** register (`none`, never a secret), and **only loopback redirect URIs**: `http://localhost`, `http://127.0.0.1` or `http://[::1]`, an explicit port and a path, at most 200 characters, no user info, query or fragment, up to five. So a link someone sends cannot make the browser deliver a code to another host. The `redirect_uri` of an authorize request must be one of them **as a whole string**: another port, a trailing slash, another case, or the same address by another name is refused, which is what Codex's random path segment needs and what no port wildcard would give. Client ID metadata documents are not supported: the server would have to fetch a URL the client names.

**The token** is `sbat_…` like any other, kept as a sha256, one live per device name and counted against the person's cap of 20, listed in Settings → Agents beside the typed ones (`signed in from Codex · made … · last used …`) and revoked by the same button, which ends the device's sessions (§49.3). Removing a person revokes theirs (§49.1), and a code made for a person who was removed in the meantime is refused. **There is no refresh token and no expiry**, like a typed token's: the hooks and Claude Code's listener hold a fixed token anyway, a rotation would add a race between the sessions on one machine, and the control is Revoke. A revoked sign-in makes the agent's next call a 401 with the metadata, and the agent asks its person to sign in again (Codex: `AuthRequired`, `codex mcp login switchboard`). A name that a token or sign-in already holds is refused, never replaced, so Claude Code and Codex on one machine sign in under two names.

**Guards.** `OAUTH_PATHS` (`auth.py`) is a second, two-path list beside `AGENT_PATHS`: `POST /oauth/register` and `POST /oauth/token` are exempt from `HostOriginGuard`'s rule that an unsafe request carry the page's Origin, because the callers are programs that send none; each instead **refuses any request that has an `Origin`** (a browser's script always does) and reads no cookie, as `/mcp` does (§49.2). The route-enumeration test (`test_no_unauthenticated_write_routes`) passes unchanged for that reason. Every other route of this section (`/oauth/authorize`, `/api/oauth/*`) keeps the full rule, and the CSP is unchanged: the consent page is a static file with a same-origin script, its Approve is a `fetch`, and the hand-off to the agent is a navigation (the policy's `form-action` does not apply). The page cannot be framed.

**Threat model delta** (to §31.6 and §49.1)
- **Nothing a room message or another agent can do approves a sign-in.** Approving is the person's click on a page that only a signed-in browser's cookie, Origin and header open: a link in a room opens the consent page and nothing more, an agent token is refused where a session is needed, and a request from another site fails the Origin rule. What the person is shown is the broker's own check of the request, not the agent's description of it.
- **A consent phishing link** (a client registers as "Claude Code" and sends the link) can only deliver its code to a loopback address on the machine the browser runs on, and the code is useless without the verifier only that client holds. The page says what approving grants and to approve only a sign-in the person just started; what is shown is the name the client gave itself and never an identity.
